她确实从未给过他一丝一毫的鼓励。哪怕失礼，她也*必须*把这番殷勤在起头就掐断。只要不到失礼的地步，她就要让他明白，这份对她举动的热切关切、这些含情的注视，是如何地讨嫌。她再不朝他看了；他在场时，她要把眼睛严严地垂下——她立下这谨慎的决心时，全然无意地一抬眼，又撞上他那耐心守候的目光。
“哎，您太苛了，乔利夫小姐，”建筑师说，“看见一个头衔落到阿纳斯塔西娅小姐头上，我们大家都高兴；而且，”他柔声补了一句，“我敢说，谁也及不上她配得那样好。”
他巴不得撇掉“小姐”这个客套的前缀，直呼她阿纳斯塔西娅。几个月前他会自然而然、不加思索地这样做；可这姑娘的神态里有了什么东西，使他近来不敢再享这份乐趣。
于是那位未定的贵妇起身出了屋。
“我去照看一下面包，”她说，“我想这会儿该烤好了。”
乔利夫小姐的顾忌终被压服，韦斯特雷留下了文件；一半是因为有人向她指出，他若不加以检视，便是对死者意愿的公然怠慢——在乔利夫小姐所属的圈子里，死者的意愿是高于一切的圣物——一半是因为占有即法律的九分，而建筑师已经把它们稳稳地锁进了自己房里。但他一时无法分神照管它们，因为他生命中一场危机正在逼近，眼下把他的心思占得满满的。
他对阿纳斯塔西娅·乔利夫怀有倾慕，已有些时日了。初初察觉这份感情时，他奋力抵抗，起初还颇见成效。他深深自觉，同乔利夫家的任何瓜葛都有损他的尊严；他怕两人地位的悬殊，即便引不来敌意的物议，也足够惹人注目。人们一定会说，一个建筑师娶房东太太的侄女，是极大地屈就。他若做出这样的事，无疑是在社会上自弃。

His father, who was dead, had been a Wesleyan pastor; and his mother, who survived, entertained so great a respect for the high position of that ministry that she had impressed upon Westray from boyhood the privileges and responsibilities of his birth. But apart from this objection, there was the further drawback that an early marriage might unduly burden him with domestic cares, and so arrest his professional progress. Such considerations had due weight with an equally-balanced mind, and Westray was soon able to congratulate himself on having effectually extinguished any dangerous inclinations by sheer strength of reason.
This happy and philosophic state of things was not of long duration. His admiration smouldered only, and was not quenched, but it was a totally extraneous influence, rather than the constant contemplation of Anastasia’s beauty and excellencies, which fanned the flame into renewed activity. This extraneous factor was the entrance of Lord Blandamer into the little circle of Bellevue Lodge. Westray had lately become doubtful as to the real object of Lord Blandamer’s visits, and nursed a latent idea that he was using the church, and the restoration, and Westray himself, to gain a pied-à-terre at Bellevue Lodge for the prosecution of other plans. The long conversations in which the architect and the munificent donor still indulged, the examination of plans, the discussion of details, had lost something of their old savour. Westray had done his best to convince himself that his own suspicions were groundless; he had continually pointed out to himself, and insisted to himself, that the mere fact of Lord Blandamer contributing such sums to the restoration as he either had contributed, or had promised to contribute, showed that the church was indeed his primary concern. It was impossible to conceive that any man, however wealthy, should spend many thousand pounds to obtain an entrée to Bellevue Lodge; moreover, it was impossible to conceive that Lord Blandamer should ever marry Anastasia﻿—the disparity in such a match would, Westray admitted, be still greater than in his own. Yet he was convinced that Anastasia was often in Lord Blandamer’s thoughts. It was true that the Master of Fording gave no definite outward sign of any predilection when Westray was present.

===Chinese===

他已故的父亲是循道宗的牧师；他的母亲尚在人世，对那个圣职的崇高地位怀着莫大的敬意，从韦斯特雷孩提时候起，就把他这出身的好处和责任铭刻在他心上。但除了这一层障碍，还有一重不便：早婚或使他过早地背上家室的操劳，滞碍他事业的前程。这样的考量在一个心志均衡的人自有其分量，韦斯特雷不久便能庆幸，自己已凭纯粹的理性力量，把一切危险的倾向有效地扑灭了。
这桩如意而达观的局面未能持久。他的倾慕只是焖着，并没有熄灭；而重新把火扇旺的，倒不是对阿纳斯塔西娅的美丽与贤德的日日凝想，而是一种全然外来的影响。这外来的因素，就是布兰达默勋爵进入了贝尔维尤别墅的小圈子。韦斯特雷新近对布兰达默勋爵来访的真实目的起了疑心，暗暗认定他是借教堂、借修缮、借韦斯特雷本人，给自己在贝尔维尤别墅谋一个落脚点，好进行别的图谋。建筑师同这位慷慨的捐主照旧沉溺其中的长谈、图纸的查验、细节的商讨，已经失去了几分旧日的滋味。韦斯特雷竭力说服自己，说自己的怀疑毫无根据；他不断地向自己指出、向自己坚称，单凭布兰达默勋爵为修缮捐出或认捐了这样的巨款这一事实，就证明教堂确是他首要的关切。无法想象一个人——无论怎样富有——会为得到贝尔维尤别墅的入门资格而花掉几千镑；况且，也无法想象布兰达默勋爵会娶阿纳斯塔西娅——韦斯特雷承认，那样一桩婚事的悬殊，比他自己的还要大。然而他确信，阿纳斯塔西娅常在布兰达默勋爵的心上。不错，福丁的主人当着韦斯特雷的面，并没有显出任何偏爱的明确外象。

===Original===

He never singled Anastasia out either for regard or conversation on such occasions as chance brought her into his company. At times he even made a show of turning away from her, of studiously neglecting her presence.
But Westray felt that the fact was there.
There is some subtle effluence of love which hovers about one who entertains a strong affection for another. Looks may be carefully guarded, speech may be framed to mislead, yet that pervading ambient of affection is strong to betray where perception is sharpened by jealousy.
Now and then the architect would persuade himself that he was mistaken; he would reproach himself with his own suspicious disposition, with his own lack of generosity. But then some little episode would occur, some wholly undemonstrable trifle, which swept his cooler judgment to the winds, and gave him a quite incommensurate heartburn. He would recall, for instance, the fact that for their interviews Lord Blandamer had commonly selected a Saturday afternoon. Lord Blandamer had explained this by saying that he was busy through the week; but then a lord was not like a schoolboy with a Saturday half-holiday. What business could he have to occupy him all the week, and leave him free on Saturdays? It was strange enough, and stranger from the fact that Miss Euphemia Joliffe was invariably occupied on that particular afternoon at the Dorcas meeting; stranger from the fact that there had been some unaccountable misunderstandings between Lord Blandamer and Westray as to the exact hour fixed for their interviews, and that more than once when the architect had returned at five, he had found that Lord Blandamer had taken four as the time of their meeting, and had been already waiting an hour at Bellevue Lodge.
Poor Mr. Sharnall also must have noticed that something was going on, for he had hinted as much to Westray a fortnight or so before he died. Westray was uncertain as to Lord Blandamer’s feelings; he gave the architect the idea of a man who had some definite object to pursue in making himself interesting to Anastasia, while his own affections were not compromised. That object could certainly not be marriage, and if it was not marriage, what was it? In ordinary cases an answer might have been easy, yet Westray hesitated to give it.

===Chinese===

机缘把阿纳斯塔西娅带到他跟前时，他也从不单挑她另眼相看或攀谈。有时他甚至做样子避开她，故意怠慢她在场。
可韦斯特雷感到那事实是在的。
爱有一种微妙的流布，萦绕在怀着深情的人身上。目光可以严加防守，言语可以布置成误导，可那弥漫周遭的温情氛围，在嫉妒磨利了感知的地方，自有力量泄露真相。
建筑师不时说服自己是他错了；他责备自己多疑的性子、责备自己缺乏雅量。可随后总有小小的插曲发生，某件全然无从指证的小事，把他冷静的判断扫到九霄云外，给他一阵全不相称的心痛。譬如他会想起，布兰达默勋爵的来访照例选在星期六下午。布兰达默勋爵解释过，说平日的整周他都很忙；可是，一位勋爵又不是只有星期六半日假的学童。他能有什么事占满整周、单单星期六得闲？这就够怪了；更怪的是，尤菲米娅·乔利夫小姐偏在那个下午必在多加会上；更怪的是，布兰达默勋爵同韦斯特雷之间，为约定的确切钟点出过几回莫名其妙的误会，不止一次，建筑师五点回来，发现布兰达默勋爵把四点当作了会面的时刻，已经在贝尔维尤别墅干等了一个钟头。
可怜的沙纳尔先生想必也察觉了什么动静，因为他死前两星期左右，曾向韦斯特雷如此这般暗示过。布兰达默勋爵的感情如何，韦斯特雷拿不准；他给建筑师的印象是：此人对阿纳斯塔西娅卖弄殷勤自有明确的目的，而他自己的感情并不搭进去。那目的必不会是婚姻；若不是婚姻，又是什么？寻常的案子里，答案或许唾手可得，韦斯特雷却迟迟不肯说出口。

===Original===

It was hard to think that this grave man, of great wealth and great position, who had roamed the world, and known men and manners, should stoop to common lures. Yet Westray came to think it, and his own feelings towards Anastasia were elevated by the resolve to be her knightly champion against all base attempts.
Can man’s deepest love be deepened? Then it must surely be by the knowledge that he is protector as well as lover, by the knowledge that he is rescuing innocence, and rescuing it for﻿—himself. Thoughts such as these bring exaltation to the humblest-minded, and they quickened the slow-flowing and thin fluid that filled the architect’s veins.
He came back one evening from the church weary with a long day’s work, and was sitting by the fire immersed in a medley of sleepy and half-conscious consideration, now of the crack in the centre tower, now of the tragedy of the organ-loft, now of Anastasia, when the elder Miss Joliffe entered.
“Dear me, sir,” she said, “I did not know you were in! I only came to see your fire was burning. Are you ready for your tea? Would you like anything special tonight? You do look so very tired. I am sure you are working too hard; all the running about on ladders and scaffolds must be very trying. I think indeed, sir, if I may make so bold, that you should take a holiday; you have not had a holiday since you came to live with us.”
“It is not impossible, Miss Joliffe, that I may take your advice before very long. It is not impossible that I may before long go for a holiday.”
He spoke with that preternatural gravity which people are accustomed to throw into their reply, if asked a trivial question when their own thoughts are secretly occupied with some matter that they consider of deep importance. How could this commonplace woman guess that he was thinking of death and love? He must be gentle with her and forgive her interruption. Yes, fate might, indeed, drive him to take a holiday. He had nearly made up his mind to propose to Anastasia. It was scarcely to be doubted that she would at once accept him, but there must be no half-measures, he would brook no shilly-shallying, he would not be played fast and loose with. She must either accept him fully and freely, and at once, or he would withdraw his offer, and in that case, or still more in the entirely improbable case of refusal, he would leave Bellevue Lodge forthwith.

===Chinese===

很难想象这样一位庄重的人，巨富而尊贵，走遍世界、阅尽人情，竟会俯就俗丽的钓饵。可韦斯特雷渐渐这样想了，而他自任为她的骑士护持，抵御一切卑鄙的图谋，他对阿纳斯塔西娅的感情也随之升高。
人的至深之爱还能再加深吗？若能，必是靠知道自己是护持者、也是爱人，是知道自己在拯救无辜——而且是为*自己*拯救。这样的念头给最谦卑的心也带来升华，它们也催快了建筑师血管里那缓慢而稀薄的血流。
一天晚上，他从教堂回来，一整天的劳作使他疲惫，坐在炉火前，浸在一团半睡半醒的乱想里——一时是中央塔的裂缝，一时是琴楼的惨剧，一时是阿纳斯塔西娅——这时老乔利夫小姐进来了。
“哎呀，先生，”她说，“我不知道您在家！我只是来看看您的火生着没有。茶预备好了吗？今晚想用点什么特别的吗？您瞧着实在累得很。我敢说您太用功了；成天在梯子上脚手架上爬上爬下，一定很劳人。我想，先生，恕我斗胆，您该歇个假了；您住到我们家来，还没歇过一回呢。”
“不是没有可能，乔利夫小姐，我不久就要听从您的劝告。不是没有可能，我不久就要去度一回假。”
他说话时带着一种超乎寻常的郑重——人心里暗暗盘踞着自认为重大的事情时，被问了句闲话，回话往往就是这样的腔调。这个寻常的女人怎么会猜到，他想的是死亡和爱情？他得对她温存些，原谅她的搅扰。是的，命运说不定真会逼他去度假。他几乎已经拿定主意向阿纳斯塔西娅求婚。她将立即应允，此事殆无可疑；但不许有半吊子的措施，他容不得拖泥带水，容不得被人耍弄。她必须充分地、爽快地、立刻地应允他；否则他就收回提议，而一旦如此——或在全然不大可能发生的被拒的情形下——他将即刻离开贝尔维尤别墅。

===Original===

“Yes, indeed, I may ere long have to go away for a holiday.”
The conscious forbearance of replying at all gave a quiet dignity to his tone, and an involuntary sigh that accompanied his words was not lost upon Miss Joliffe. To her this speech seemed oracular and ominous; there was a sepulchral mystery in so vague an expression. He might *have* to take a holiday. What could this mean? Was this poor young man completely broken by the loss of his friend Mr. Sharnall, or was he conscious of the seeds of some fell disease that others knew nothing of? He might *have to* take a holiday. Ah, it was not a mere holiday of which he spoke﻿—he meant something more serious than that; his grave, sad manner could only mean some long absence. Perhaps he was going to leave Cullerne.
To lose him would be a very serious matter to Miss Joliffe from the material point of view; he was her sheet-anchor, the last anchor that kept Bellevue Lodge from drifting into bankruptcy. Mr. Sharnall was dead, and with him had died the tiny pittance which he contributed to the upkeep of the place, and lodgers were few and far between in Cullerne. Miss Joliffe might well have remembered these things, but she did not. The only thought that crossed her mind was that if Mr. Westray went away she would lose yet another friend. She did not approach the matter from the material point of view, she looked on him only as a friend; she viewed him as no moneymaking machine, but only as that most precious of all treasures﻿—a last friend.
“I may have to leave you for awhile,” he said again, with the same portentous solemnity.
“I hope not, sir,” she interrupted, as though by her very eagerness she might avert threatened evil﻿—“I hope not; we should miss you terribly, Mr. Westray, with dear Mr. Sharnall gone too. I do not know what we should do having no man in the house. It is so very lonely if you are away even for a night. I am an old woman now, and it does not matter much for me, but Anastasia is so nervous at night since the dreadful accident.”
Westray’s face brightened a little at the mention of Anastasia’s name. Yes, his must certainly be a very deep affection, that the naming of her very name should bring him such pleasure. It was on *his* protection, then, that she leant; she looked on *him* as her defender. The muscles of his not gigantic arms seemed to swell and leap to bursting in his coat-sleeves.

===Chinese===

“是的，当真，我不久也许就得离家度假了。”
连答话本身所含的自制，都给他的语调添了一种安静的尊严，伴随话语的一声不由自主的叹息，也没有逃过乔利夫小姐的耳朵。在她听来，这话如同神谕，凶吉难测；这样一个含糊的说法里有坟墓般的神秘。他也许*不得不*度假。这能是什么意思呢？是这位可怜的年轻人被丧友之痛压垮了，还是他自觉种下了别人一无所知的恶疾？他也许*不得不*度假。啊，他说的绝不是寻常的度假——他指的是更严重的事；他那沉重哀伤的神态，只能意味着长久的别离。也许他要离开卡勒恩了。
失去他，对乔利夫小姐将是一件物质上的大事；他是她的宿锚，是使贝尔维尤别墅不致漂向破产的最后一枚锚。沙纳尔先生死了，他对这宅子维持费的微薄贴补也随他死了；而卡勒恩的房客本来就稀少。乔利夫小姐本可把这些都记在心上，可她没有。掠过她脑际的唯一念头是：韦斯特雷先生若走了，她又要失去一位朋友。她不从物质上考虑这件事，只把他当一位朋友看；她不把他看作赚钱的机器，只把他看作万般珍宝中最珍贵的那一样——最后一位朋友。
“我也许要离开你们一阵子。”他又说了一遍，还是那种凶多吉少的郑重。
“我希望不会，先生，”她插嘴道，仿佛单凭这一腔热切就能驱走逼来的祸事——“我希望不会；亲爱的沙纳尔先生又去了，您再走，我们会想念得不得了的。屋里没有个男人，真不知我们该怎么办。哪怕您只外出一夜，也冷清得很。我如今是个老太婆了，怎么都不要紧；可自从那桩可怕的事故，阿纳斯塔西娅夜里胆子小得很。”
提到阿纳斯塔西娅的名字，韦斯特雷的脸微微亮了。是了，他的爱必定极深，单单提一提她的名字便给他这样的快乐。原来她倚的是*他*的保护；她把*他*看作自己的护卫。他那算不得粗壮的手臂的肌肉，似乎在衣袖里鼓胀起来，要撑裂了。

===Original===

Those arms should screen his loved one from all evil. Visions of Perseus, and Sir Galahad, and Cophetua, swept before his eyes; he had almost cried to Miss Euphemia, “You need have no fear, I love your niece. I shall bow down and raise her to my throne. They that would touch her shall only do so over my dead body,” when hesitating common sense plucked him by the sleeve; he must consult his mother before taking this grave step.
It was well that reason thus restrained him, for such a declaration might have brought Miss Joliffe to a swoon. As it was, she noticed the cloud lifting on his face, and was pleased to think that her conversation cheered him. A little company was no doubt good for him, and she sought in her mind for some further topic of interest. Yes, of course, she had it.
“Lord Blandamer was here this afternoon. He came just like anyone else might have come, in such a very kind and condescending way to ask after me. He feared that dear Mr. Sharnall’s death might have been too severe a shock for us both, and, indeed, it has been a terrible blow. He was so considerate, and sat for nearly an hour﻿—for forty-seven minutes I should say by the clock, and took tea with us in the kitchen as if he were one of the family. I never could have expected such condescension, and when he went away he left a most polite message for you, sir, to say that he was sorry that you were not in, but he hoped to call again before long.”
The cloud had returned to Westray’s face. If he had been the hero of a novel his brow would have been black as night; as it was he only looked rather sulky.
“I shall have to go to London tonight,” he said stiffly, without acknowledging Miss Joliffe’s remarks; “I shall not be back tomorrow, and may be away a few days. I will write to let you know when I shall be back.”
Miss Joliffe started as if she had received an electric shock.
“To London tonight,” she began﻿—“this very night?”
“Yes,” Westray said, with a dryness that would have suggested of itself that the interview was to be terminated, even if he had not added: “I shall be glad to be left alone now; I have several letters to write before I can get away.”
So Miss Euphemia went to impart this strange matter to the maiden who was *ex hypothesi* leaning on the architect’s strong arm.
“What *do* you think, Anastasia?” she said. “Mr. Westray is going to London tonight, perhaps for some days.”

===Chinese===

那双手臂要为他所爱的人遮挡一切灾殃。珀修斯、加勒哈德爵士、科菲图亚王的身影从他眼前掠过；他几乎要向尤菲米娅小姐喊出来：“您什么都不必怕，我爱您的侄女。我要拜倒在她面前，把她扶上我的宝座。谁想碰她一根毫毛，先得从我的尸体上跨过去。”这时踌躇的常识拽了拽他的衣袖：走这一大步之前，得先请教他的母亲。
幸而理智这样拦住了他，因为这样一句宣言准会把乔利夫小姐吓晕过去。即便如此，她也察觉他脸上的阴云散了些，很高兴自己的谈话给了他安慰。有人作伴想必对他有好处，她在心里搜寻着另一个有趣的话题。有了，当然，有了。
“布兰达默勋爵今天下午来过。他来得就像随便哪个人一样，那样和气、那样屈尊地来问候我。他担心亲爱的沙纳尔先生的死，对我们两人怕是太重的打击；这确实是一记可怕的打击。他那样体贴，坐了将近一个钟头——照钟算，我该说坐了四十七分钟——还在厨房同我们一起用了茶，就像自家人一样。这样的屈尊我做梦也想不到；临走时，他还给您留了一句极客气的话，先生，说很遗憾您不在家，他希望不久再来拜访。”
阴云又回到韦斯特雷脸上。他若是小说里的英雄，额头早黑得像夜了；实际上他只是显得相当愠怒。
“我今晚得去伦敦，”他生硬地说，对乔利夫小姐的话不置一词；“明天不回来，也许要走几天。回来的时候我写信告诉您。”
乔利夫小姐一惊，像挨了电击。
“今晚去伦敦，”她说——“就是今夜？”
“是的。”韦斯特雷说，那干巴巴的腔调本身就表明谈话该结束了，纵然他没有加上那句：“现在我想一个人待着；动身前还有几封信要写。”
于是尤菲米娅去把这桩奇事告诉那位——按假说——正倚在建筑师强壮手臂上的少女了。
“你猜怎么着，阿纳斯塔西娅？”她说，“韦斯特雷先生今晚要去伦敦，也许要去好几天。”

===Original===

“Is he?” was all her niece’s comment; but there was a languor and indifference in the voice, that might have sent the thermometer of the architect’s affection from boiling-point to below blood-heat, if he could have heard her speak.
Westray sat moodily for a few moments after his landlady had gone. For the first time in his life he wished he was a smoker. He wished he had a pipe in his mouth, and could pull in and puff out smoke as he had seen Sharnall do when *he* was moody. He wanted some work for his restless body while his restless mind was turning things over. It was the news of Lord Blandamer’s visit, as on this very afternoon, that fanned smouldering thoughts into flame. This was the first time, so far as Westray knew, that Lord Blandamer had come to Bellevue Lodge without at least a formal excuse of business. With that painful effort which we use to convince ourselves of things of which we wish to be convinced in the face of all difficulties; with that blind, stumbling hope against hope with which we try to reconcile things irreconcilable, if only by so doing we can conjure away a haunting spectre, or lull to sleep a bitter suspicion; the architect had hitherto resolved to believe that if Lord Blandamer came with some frequency to Bellevue Lodge, he was only prompted to do so by a desire to keep in touch with the restoration, to follow with intelligence the expenditure of money which he was so lavishly providing. It had been the easier for Westray to persuade himself that Lord Blandamer’s motives were legitimate, because he felt that the other must find a natural attraction in the society of a talented young professional man. An occasional conversation with a clever architect on things architectural, or on other affairs of common interest (for Westray was careful to avoid harping unduly on any single topic) must undoubtedly prove a relief to Lord Blandamer from the monotony of bachelor life in the country; and in such considerations Westray found a subsidiary, and sometimes he was inclined to imagine primary, interest for these visits to Bellevue Lodge.
If various circumstances had conspired of late to impugn the sufficiency of these motives, Westray had not admitted as much in his own mind; if he had been disquieted, he had constantly assured himself that disquietude was unreasonable. But now disillusion had befallen him.

===Chinese===

“是吗？”她侄女应了一句；可那话音里的倦怠和淡漠，倘若建筑师听得见，怕要让他的爱情温度从沸点直落到体温之下。
房东太太走后，韦斯特雷郁郁地坐了片刻。他有生以来头一回巴望自己是个抽烟的人。他巴不得嘴里衔着烟斗，能像沙纳尔心境不好时那样吞云吐雾。他不安的心思翻搅不停，需要给不安的身体寻点活计。正是布兰达默勋爵来访——就在今天下午——的消息，把焖着的念头扇成了明火。据韦斯特雷所知，这是布兰达默勋爵头一回连事务的表面借口都不要，径直来到贝尔维尤别墅。人要说服自己去相信那些尽管万难、却宁肯相信的事时，用的正是那种吃力的功夫；人要调和那无可调和的事，只求借此驱走缠身的鬼影、哄睡那苦涩的疑心时，用的正是那种盲目的、跌跌撞撞的死里求生的指望；建筑师迄今便这样认定：布兰达默勋爵若来得频繁，也只因他想与修缮声气相通，想用心追踪他那般挥霍供给的款项的用途。韦斯特雷所以容易说服自己相信布兰达默勋爵动机纯正，还因为他觉得，对方在同一位有才华的年轻专业人士交往中，必能寻得天然的吸力。同一位聪明的建筑师偶尔谈谈建筑、谈谈旁的共同关心之事（因为韦斯特雷留意着不过分纠缠任何单一的题目），对布兰达默勋爵无疑是一种调剂，解一解乡间鳏居生活的单调；在这样的考量里，韦斯特雷为这些来访找到了一个附带——有时他简直倾向于认为是主要——的理由。
近来的种种情事虽合力非难这些动机的充分性，韦斯特雷内心却从未承认；他纵有过不安，也不断向自己担保，不安是没有道理的。可如今幻灭临头了。

===Original===

Lord Blandamer had visited Bellevue Lodge as it were in his own right; he had definitely abandoned the pretence of coming to see Westray; he had been drinking tea with Miss Joliffe; he had spent an hour in the kitchen with Miss Joliffe and﻿—Anastasia. It could only mean one thing, and Westray’s resolution was taken.
An object which had seemed at best but mildly desirable, became of singular value when he believed that another was trying to possess himself of it; jealousy had quickened love, duty and conscience insisted that he should save the girl from the snare that was being set for her. The great renunciation must be made; he, Westray, must marry beneath him, but before doing so he would take his mother into his confidence, though there is no record of Perseus doing as much before he cut loose Andromeda.
Meanwhile, no time must be lost; he would start this very night. The last train for London had already left, but he would walk to Cullerne Road Station and catch the night-mail from thence. He liked walking, and need take no luggage, for there were things that he could use at his mother’s house. It was seven o’clock when he came to this resolve, and an hour later he had left the last house in Cullerne behind him, and entered upon his night excursion.
The line of the Roman way which connected Carauna (Carisbury) with its port Culurnum (Cullerne) is still followed by the modern road, and runs as nearly straight as may be for the sixteen miles which separate those places. About halfway between them the Great Southern main line crosses the highway at right angles, and here is Cullerne Road Station. The first half of the way runs across a flat sandy tract called Mallory Heath, where the short greensward encroaches on the road, and where the eye roaming east or west or north can discern nothing except a limitless expanse of heather, broken here and there by patches of gorse and bracken, or by clumps of touselled and wind-thinned pines and Scotch firs. The tawny-coloured, sandy track is difficult to follow in the dark, and there are posts set up at intervals on the skirts of the way for travellers’ guidance. These posts show out white against a starless night, and dark against the snow which sometimes covers the heath with a silvery sheet.

===Chinese===

布兰达默勋爵以自己的身份径直来访贝尔维尤别墅；他明白放弃了来看韦斯特雷的借口；他同乔利夫小姐喝了茶；他在厨房里同乔利夫小姐和——阿纳斯塔西娅——消磨了一个钟头。这只能意味着一件事，韦斯特雷拿定了主意。
一样东西，原本至多是差强人意的想望，一旦他相信另一个人正想把它据为己有，便有了奇异的价值；嫉妒催快了爱情，天职和良心坚持要他把姑娘从那为她设下的圈套里救出来。伟大的舍身必须做出；他韦斯特雷必须屈尊下娶；但在这样做之前，他要向母亲推心置腹——虽然并无记载说珀修斯解开安德洛墨达之前也曾如此。
而且，事不宜迟；他当夜就动身。去伦敦的末班车已经开走了，可他可以步行到卡勒恩路站，从那里搭夜班邮车。他喜欢走路，行李也不必带，母亲家里有用得着的东西。他下定这个决心时是七点钟；一个钟头后，他已经把卡勒恩最后一所房子抛在身后，踏上了他的夜行。
连接卡劳纳（卡里斯伯里）与其港口库卢努姆（卡勒恩）的罗马大道的路线，如今仍为近代的公路所循，在那两处之间相隔的十六英里上，直得不能再直。大约中途，大南方铁路干线以直角横过公路，卡勒恩路站就在这里。前半程要穿过一片平坦的沙地，唤作马洛里荒原；短短的草皮侵上路面，放眼东西北三面，除了望不到头的石南，什么也看不见，只是间或被一片金雀花和蕨草、或一丛丛蓬乱风瘦的松树和苏格兰冷杉割断。这条黄褐色的沙径夜里难行，沿途隔一程竖着标杆为旅人指路。在没有星星的夜里，标杆衬得发白；大雪把荒原盖成银毯时，又衬得发黑。

===Original===

On a clear night the traveller can see the far-off lamps of the station at Cullerne Road a mile after he has left the old seaport town. They stand out like a thin line of light in the distant darkness, a line continuous at first, but afterwards resolvable into individual units of lamps as he walks further along the straight road. Many a weary wayfarer has watched those lamps hang changeless in the distance, and chafed at their immobility. They seem to come no nearer to him for all the milestones, with the distance from Hyde Park Corner graven in old figures on their lichened faces, that he has passed. Only the increasing sound of the trains tells him that he is nearing his goal, and by degrees the dull rumble becomes a clanking roar as the expresses rush headlong by. On a crisp winter day they leave behind them a trail of whitest wool, and in the nighttime a fiery serpent follows them when the open furnace-door flings on the cloud a splendid radiance. But in the dead heats of midsummer the sun dries up the steam, and they speed along, the more wonderful because there is no trace to tell what power it is that drives them.
Of all these things Westray saw nothing. A soft white fog had fallen upon everything. It drifted by in delicate whirling wreaths, that seemed to have an innate motion of their own where all had been still but a minute before. It covered his clothes with a film of the finest powdery moisture that ran at a touch into heavy drops, it hung in dripping dew on his moustache, and hair, and eyebrows, it blinded him, and made him catch his breath. It had come rolling in from the sea as on that night when Mr. Sharnall was taken, and Westray could hear the distant groaning of foghorns in the Channel; and looking backwards towards Cullerne, knew from a blurred glare, now green, now red, that a vessel in the offing was signalling for a coastwise pilot. He plodded steadily forward, stopping now and then when he found his feet on the grass sward to recover the road, and rejoicing when one of the white posts assured him that he was still keeping the right direction. The blinding fog isolated him in a strange manner; it cut him off from Nature, for he could see nothing of her; it cut him off from man, for he could not have seen even a legion of soldiers had they surrounded him.

===Chinese===

晴夜里，旅人离开那座老海港城一英里后，便望得见卡勒恩路站远处的灯火。它们像远处黑暗里一线细长的光，起初连成一线，待他顺着直路走得远了，便分解成一盏一盏的灯。多少疲惫的行客望着那些灯远远地悬着不动，为它们的纹丝不动而恼火。他走过一块又一块里程碑——碑面上苔痕斑斑，刻着到海德公园角的旧里数——灯却似乎毫不靠近。只有越来越响的火车声告诉他终点在望，闷闷的轰隆渐渐变成铿锵的咆哮，快车一头冲过。干冷的冬日，它们身后拖一缕雪白的绒毛；黑夜里，敞开的炉门把灿烂的光投上云天，一条火蛇随车而行。可在盛夏的酷暑里，太阳吸干了蒸汽，它们疾驰而过，因为没有一丝痕迹说明是什么力量在驱驰，反而更加神奇。
这一切韦斯特雷一样也没有看见。一层柔白的雾罩下了一切。雾一绺一绺地打着旋飘过，一分钟前还是静止的地方，它们却仿佛自有先天的动静。它给他的衣裳蒙上一层最细的粉状水膜，手一碰便化作沉甸甸的水珠；它化作滴沥的露挂在他的髭须、头发和眉毛上；它迷了他的眼，逼得他屏住呼吸。雾是从海上滚滚而来的，像沙纳尔先生遭难的那夜一样；韦斯特雷听得见海峡里雾号遥远的呻吟；回望卡勒恩方向，凭一片时绿时红的模糊光晕，他知道外海上有一艘船在打信号，招引近海领航员。他稳步向前，不时发觉脚下踩上草皮，便停下来找回路；每逢一根白标杆向他保证方向未错，他便一阵欢喜。这迷眼的雾古怪地把他隔绝开来；它把他同自然隔开，因为自然他一无所见；它把他同人隔开，因为纵有一军团的大兵围住他，他也看不见。

===Original===

This removal of outside influences threw him back upon himself, and delivered him to introspection; he began for the hundredth time to weigh his position, to consider whether the momentous step that he was taking was necessary to his ease of mind, was right, was prudent.
To make a proposal of marriage is a matter that may give the strongest-minded pause, and Westray’s mind was not of the strongest. He was clever, imaginative, obstinate, scrupulous to a fault; but had not that broad outlook on life which comes of experience, nor the power and resolution to readily take a decision under difficult circumstances, and to abide by it once taken. So it was that reason made a shuttlecock of his present resolve, and half a dozen times he stopped in the road meaning to abandon his purpose, and turn back to Cullerne. Yet half a dozen times he went on, though with slow feet, thinking always, Was he right in what he was doing, was he right? And the fog grew thicker; it seemed almost to be stifling him; he could not see his hand if he held it at arm’s length before his face. Was he right, was there any right or any wrong, was anything real, was not everything subjective﻿—the creation of his own brain? Did he exist, was he himself, was he in the body or out of the body? And then a wild dismay, a horror of the darkness and the fog, seized hold of him. He stretched out his arms, and groped in the mist as if he hoped to lay hold of someone, or something, to reassure him as to his own identity, and at last a mind-panic got the better of him; he turned and started back to Cullerne.
It was only for a moment, and then reason began to recover her sway; he stopped, and sat down on the heather at the side of the road, careless that every spray was wet and dripping, and collected his thoughts. His heart was beating madly as in one that wakes from a nightmare, but he was now ashamed of his weakness and of the mental debacle, though there had been none to see it. What could have possessed him, what madness was this? After a few minutes he was able to turn round once more, and resumed his walk towards the railway with a firm, quick step, which should prove to his own satisfaction that he was master of himself.

===Chinese===

外界影响的撤除把他掷回自身，交付给内省；他第一百次掂量自己的处境，思量这重大的一步，于心境的安宁是否必要、是否正当、是否明智。
求婚这件事，足以叫最强的头脑也踌躇，而韦斯特雷的头脑不属于最强之列。他聪明，富于想象，固执，精细得过分；却没有经验养成的开阔眼界，也没有在困境中当机立断、断而不移的魄力与决心。于是理性把他眼下的决心当羽毛球打，他半打次在路中停步，想放弃此行，折回卡勒恩。可半打次他又走了下去，虽然脚步迟缓，心里始终想着：他所做的对吗？他对吗？雾越来越浓，简直要使他窒息；手臂伸直举在脸前，也看不见自己的手。他对吗？有对有错吗？有实在的东西吗？一切岂不都是主观的——是他自己头脑的造物？他存在吗？他是他自己吗？他在身内还是身外？随后一阵狂乱的惊惶、一阵对黑暗和浓雾的恐怖攫住了他。他伸出双臂，在雾里摸索，仿佛指望抓住什么人或什么东西，来担保他自己的存在；末了，一场心里的恐慌占了上风，他转身往卡勒恩跑。
不过是一瞬，理性便开始收复失地；他停住，在路边的石南丛上坐下，顾不得每一枝都湿得滴水，收拾起自己的思绪。他的心狂跳着，像从梦魇中醒来的人；虽然无人看见，他此刻却为自己的软弱、为这场精神的溃败羞惭。什么东西附了他体？这是怎样的痴狂？几分钟后，他便能重新转过身去，迈着坚定急速的脚步继续向车站行进——这脚步该向他本人证明：他是自己的主人。

===Original===

For the rest of his journey he dismissed bewildering questions of right and wrong, of prudence and imprudence, laying it down as an axiom that his emprise was both right and prudent, and busied himself with the more material and homely considerations of ways and means. He amused himself in attempting to fix the sum for which it would be possible for him and Anastasia to keep house, and by mentally straining to the utmost the resources at his command managed to make them approach his estimate. Another man in similar circumstances might perhaps have given himself to reviewing the chances of success in his proposal, but Westray did not trouble himself with any doubts on this point. It was a foregone conclusion that if he once offered himself Anastasia would accept him; she could not be so oblivious to the advantages which such a marriage would offer, both in material considerations and in the connection with a superior family. He only regarded the matter from his own standpoint; once he was convinced that *he* cared enough for Anastasia to make her an offer, then he was sure that she would accept him.
It was true that he could not, on the spur of the moment, recollect many instances in which she had openly evinced a predilection for him, but he was conscious that she thought well of him, and she was no doubt too modest to make manifest, feelings which she could never under ordinary circumstances hope to see returned. Yet he certainly *had* received encouragement of a quiet and unobtrusive kind, quite sufficient to warrant the most favourable conclusions. He remembered how many, many times their eyes had met when they were in one another’s company; she must certainly have read the tenderness which had inspired his glances, and by answering them she had given perhaps the greatest encouragement that true modesty would permit. How delicate and infinitely gracious her acknowledgment had been, how often had she looked at him as it were furtively, and then, finding his passionate gaze upon her, had at once cast her own eyes shyly to the ground! And in his reveries he took not into reckoning, the fact that through these later weeks he had scarcely ever taken his gaze off her, so long as she was in the same room with him.

===Chinese===

余下的路程里，他把是非、智不智这些搅人的问题统统抛开，把“此行既正当又明智”立为公理，专心去考虑更实际、更家常的经费问题。他自娱地试着测算，他同阿纳斯塔西娅成家，多大的一笔款项过得下去；又把手头的财力在心里拉到极限，居然拉得接近了他的概算。换一个人处此境地，也许会去掂量求婚的成败，韦斯特雷却不为此费一点心。他一旦开口，阿纳斯塔西娅必应允，这是注定的结局；她不会看不见这样一门婚事的好处，物质上的种种实惠且不说，还有同高门第人家的结亲。他只从自己的立场看这件事；一旦他认定*他*对阿纳斯塔西娅的情意足以开口求婚，那他便确信她会应允。
的确，一时之间他想不起多少实例，说明她公开流露过对他的偏爱；可他自觉她对他颇有好评，而她无疑过于娴静，不肯把寻常情况下无望得到回应的感情表露出来。然而他*确曾*得到过一种安静而不惹眼的鼓励，尽够担保最乐观的结论。

===Original===

It would have been strange if their eyes had not sometimes met, because she must needs now and then obey that impulse which forces us to look at those who are looking at us. Certainly, he meditated, her eyes had given him encouragement, and then she had accepted gratefully a bunch of lilies of the valley which he said lightly had been given him, but which he had really bought *ad hoc* at Carisbury. But, again, he ought perhaps to have reflected that it would have been difficult for her to refuse them. How could she have refused them? How could any girl under the circumstances do less than take with thanks a few lilies of the valley? To decline them would be affectation; by declining she might attach a false and ridiculous significance to a kindly act. Yes, she had encouraged him in the matter of the lilies, and if she had not worn some of them in her bosom, as he had hoped she might, that, no doubt, was because she feared to show her preference too markedly. He had noticed particularly the interest she had shown when a bad cold had confined him for a few days to the house, and this very evening had he not heard that she missed him when he was absent even for a night? He smiled at this thought, invisibly in the fog; and has not a man a right to some complacence, on whose presence in the house hang a fair maiden’s peace and security? Miss Joliffe had said that Anastasia felt nervous whenever he, Westray, was away; it was very possible that Anastasia had given her aunt a hint that she would like him to be told this, and he smiled again in the fog; he certainly need have no fear of any rejection of his suit.
He had been so deeply immersed in these reassuring considerations that he walked steadily on unconscious of all exterior objects and conditions until he saw the misty lights of the station, and knew that his goal was reached. His misgivings and tergiversations had so much delayed him by the way, that it was past midnight, and the train was already due. There were no other travellers on the platform, or in the little waiting-room where a paraffin-lamp with blackened chimney struggled feebly with the fog.

===Chinese===

他记得，两人同处的时候，他们的目光相遇过多少多少回；他目光里倾注的柔情，她必定读出来了；她以回望作答，这已是真正的娴静所能容许的最大鼓励。她的承认多么微妙、多么有无限的风致！她多少次仿佛偷偷地望他，一旦发觉他热烈的目光钉在自己身上，又立刻羞怯地垂下眼去！在他的痴想里，他不曾计入这样一桩事实：这几个星期以来，只要同处一室，他的目光几乎没有离开过她。他们的目光若不偶尔相遇，那才怪呢，因为她不免时时服从那股冲动——有人望着我们时，我们非望那人不可。的确，他沉吟道，她的眼睛给过他鼓励；她还感激地收下过一束铃兰——他轻描淡写说是人家送的，其实是他在卡里斯伯里*专程*买来的。不过，他也该想到，她实在难以拒绝。她怎么拒绝得了呢？换哪个姑娘，处此境地，除了道谢收下一束铃兰，还能怎样？推辞反倒显得矫情；一推辞，倒像给一桩好意安上了虚假而可笑的深意。是的，铃兰那一节她是鼓励了他的；她虽没有如他指望的那样摘几朵别在胸前，无疑只是怕把偏爱表露得太露骨。他因重伤风困居家中的那几天，她表现的关切他特别注意到了；就在今晚，他不还听说，他哪怕只外宿一夜她也惦记？想到这里他在雾里无声地笑了；一个青年女子的安宁和保障竟系于他之在堂，这样的人岂能没有几分自得？乔利夫小姐说过，韦斯特雷不在时阿纳斯塔西娅便心里发慌；很可能，是阿纳斯塔西娅自己授意姨母，让她把这话转告他的；他又在雾里笑了；他实在无需怕求婚遭拒。

===Original===

It was not a cheery room, and he was glad to be called back from a contemplation of a roll of texts hanging on the wall, and a bottle of stale water on the table, to human things by the entry of a drowsy official who was discharging the duties of stationmaster, booking-clerk, and porter all at once.
“Are you waiting for the London train, sir?” he asked in a surprised tone, that showed that the night-mail found few passengers at Cullerne Road. “She will be in now in a few minutes; have you your ticket?”
They went together to the booking-office. The stationmaster handed him a third-class ticket, without even asking how he wished to travel.
“Ah, thank you,” Westray said, “but I think I will go first-class tonight. I shall be more likely to have a compartment to myself, and shall be less disturbed by people getting in and out.”
“Certainly, sir,” said the stationmaster, with the marked increase of respect due to a first-class passenger﻿—“certainly, sir; please give me back the other ticket. I shall have to write you one﻿—we do not keep them ready; we are so very seldom asked for first-class at this station.”
“No, I suppose not,” Westray said.
“Things happen funny,” the stationmaster remarked while he got his pen. “I wrote one by this same train a month ago, and before that I don’t think we have ever sold one since the station was opened.”
“Ah,” Westray said, paying little attention, for he was engaged in a new mental disputation as to whether he was really justified in travelling first-class. He had just settled that at such a life-crisis as he had now reached, it was necessary that the body should be spared fatigue in order that the mind might be as vigorous as possible for dealing with a difficult situation, and that the extra expense was therefore justified; when the stationmaster went on:
“Yes, I wrote a ticket, just as I might for you, for Lord Blandamer not a month ago. Perhaps you know Lord Blandamer?” he added venturously; yet with a suggestion that even the sodality of first-class travelling was not in itself a passport to so distinguished an acquaintance. The mention of Lord Blandamer’s name gave a galvanic shock to Westray’s flagging attention.
“Oh yes,” he said, “I know Lord Blandamer.”
“Do you, indeed, sir”﻿—and respect had risen by a skip greater than any allowed in counterpoint.

===Chinese===

他深深浸在这些宽慰的考量里，稳步前行，外界的一切物件和境况全然不觉，直到望见车站朦胧的灯火，知道目的地到了。路上的疑惧和反复耽搁了他许久，这时已过午夜，火车也快到了。站台上再没有别的旅客；小候车室里，一盏烟囱熏黑的煤油灯同浓雾苦苦相持。那不是间叫人愉快的屋子；他正对着墙上一卷悬挂的经句和桌上一瓶走了气的水出神，幸而有位睡眼惺忪的职员进来，把他唤回到人间——此人一身兼任站长、售票员和搬运夫。
“您是等去伦敦的火车吧，先生？”他问，语气惊讶，可见夜班邮车在卡勒恩路上少有旅客。“几分钟内就进站了；票买好了吗？”
两人一同去售票处。站长递给他一张三等票，连他愿坐几等都没有问。
“啊，谢谢，”韦斯特雷说，“不过今晚我想坐头等。更容易一个人占一间隔间，也少受旅客上上下下的打扰。”
“当然，先生，”站长说，对头等旅客应有的敬意顿时加了几分——“当然，先生；请把那张票还我。我得给您手写一张——现成的我们不备；在本站，头等票实在极少有人问津。”
“我想也是。”韦斯特雷说。
“世事就有这么巧，”站长取笔时说道，“一个月前，也是这班车，我写过一张；再往前，我看自打车站开办以来就没卖出过一张。”
“啊。”韦斯特雷说，没大留意，因为他正忙于一场新的内心争辩：坐头等是否果真名正言顺。他刚刚裁定：处在眼下这样的人生危机里，必须使身体免于劳顿，好让心智以最充沛的精力应付艰难的局面，因此多花的钱是正当的；站长接着说了下去：
“是啊，我写过一张票，就像马上要给您写的一样，是给布兰达默勋爵的，还不到一个月呢。也许您认识布兰达默勋爵？”他试探着加了一句，言下带一点暗示：单凭同坐头等这一层香火情，还够不上攀这样显贵的相识。布兰达默勋爵的名字给了韦斯特雷萎靡的注意力一记电击。
“哦，认识，”他说，“我认识布兰达默勋爵。”
“真的吗，先生”——敬意又跳高了一级，比对位法里许可的跳进还大。“嗯，我给爵爷大人写票，就是这班车，还不到一个月；不，不止一个月了，因为那正是卡勒恩那位可怜的风琴师出事的那天夜里。”
“是吗，”韦斯特雷装出淡漠的样子说；“布兰达默勋爵是从哪儿来的？”
“不知道，”站长答道——“我*不*知道，先生。”他重复道，带着没有教养或没有头脑的人惯有的那种不必要的重读。
“他是坐车来的吗？”
“不，他步行到本站来的，就跟您一样。对不起，先生，”他岔开话头，“车来了。”
他们听见火车驶近的远处雷鸣，正好赶上看站台尽头的平交道闸门像被鬼手推着，悄无声息地荡开，红色信号灯随即拦断了卡勒恩公路。

===Original===

“Well, I wrote a ticket for his lordship by this very train not a month ago; no, it was not a month ago, for ’twas the very night the poor organist at Cullerne was took.”
“Yes,” said the would-be indifferent Westray; “where did Lord Blandamer come from?”
“I do not know,” the stationmaster replied﻿—“I do *not* know, sir,” he repeated, with the unnecessary emphasis common to the uneducated or unintelligent.
“Was he driving?”
“No, he walked up to this station just as you might yourself. Excuse me, sir,” he broke off; “here she comes.”
They heard the distant thunder of the approaching train, and were in time to see the gates of the level-crossing at the end of the platform swing silently open as if by ghostly hands, till their red lanterns blocked the Cullerne Road.
No one got out, and no one but Westray got in; there was some interchanging of post-office bags in the fog, and then the stationmaster-booking-clerk-porter waved a lamp, and the train steamed away. Westray found himself in a cavernous carriage, of which the cloth seats were cold and damp as the lining of a coffin. He turned up the collar of his coat, folded his arms in a Napoleonic attitude, and threw himself back into a corner to think. It was curious﻿—it was very curious. He had been under the impression that Lord Blandamer had left Cullerne early on the night of poor Sharnall’s accident; Lord Blandamer had told them at Bellevue Lodge that he was going away by the afternoon train when he left them. Yet here he was at Cullerne Road at midnight, and if he had not come from Cullerne, whence had he come? He could not have come from Fording, for from Fording he would certainly have taken the train at Lytchett. It was curious, and while he was so thinking he fell asleep.

===Chinese===

没有人下车；上车的也只有韦斯特雷一个；雾里交换了几个邮袋，随后这位站长兼售票员兼搬运夫挥了挥灯，火车喷着汽开走了。韦斯特雷发现自己置身一节洞穴般的车厢，绒面座椅又冷又潮，像棺材的衬里。他竖起大衣领子，双臂交叠摆出拿破仑的姿态，把身子向角落里一仰，思索起来。奇怪——太奇怪了。他原先的印象是，可怜的沙纳尔出事的那天晚上，布兰达默勋爵一早便离开了卡勒恩；布兰达默勋爵离开贝尔维尤别墅时，还对他们说，要搭午后的火车走。可他半夜里竟在卡勒恩路站；若不是从卡勒恩来的，他又从哪儿来？他不可能来自福丁，因为从福丁来，他一定在利切特上车。真奇怪；他正想着，便睡着了。

## XVI / 十六
===Original===

A day or two later Miss Joliffe said to Anastasia:
“I think you had a letter from Mr. Westray this morning, my dear, had you not? Did he say anything about his return? Did he say when he was coming back?”
“No, dear aunt, he said nothing about coming back. He only wrote a few lines on a matter of business.”
“Oh yes, just so,” Miss Joliffe said dryly, feeling a little hurt at what seemed like any lack of confidence on her niece’s part.
Miss Joliffe would have said that she knew Anastasia’s mind so well that no secrets were hid from her. Anastasia would have said that her aunt knew everything except a few *little* secrets, and, as a matter of fact, the one perhaps knew as much of the other as it is expedient that age should know of youth. “The mind is its own place, and in itself can make a hell of heaven, a heaven of hell.” Of all earthly consolations this is the greatest, that the mind is its own place. The mind is an impregnable fortress which can be held against all comers, the mind is a sanctuary open day or night to the pursued, the mind is a flowery pleasance where shade refreshes even in summer droughts. To some trusted friend we try to give the clue of the labyrinth, but the ball of silk is too short to guide any but ourselves along all the way. There are sunny mountaintops, there are innocent green arbours, or closes of too highly-perfumed flowers, or dank dungeons of despair, or guilty *mycethmi* black as night, where we walk alone, whither we may lead no one with us by the hand.
Miss Euphemia Joliffe would have liked to ignore altogether the matter of Westray’s letter, and to have made no further remarks thereon; but curiosity is in woman a stronger influence than pride, and curiosity drove her to recur to the letter.
“Thank you, my dear, for explaining about it. I am sure you will tell me if there are any messages for me in it.”
“No, there was no message at all for you, I think,” said Anastasia. “I will get it for you by-and-by, and you shall see all he says;” and with that she left the room as if to fetch the letter. It was only a subterfuge, for she felt Westray’s correspondence burning a hole in her pocket all the while; but she was anxious that her aunt should not see the letter until an answer to it had been posted; and hoped that if she once escaped from the room, the matter would drop out of memory.

===Chinese===

一两天后，乔利夫小姐对安娜斯塔西娅说：
「亲爱的，我想你今天早上收到韦斯特雷先生一封信吧，是不是？他说没说回来的事？说没说他什么时候回来？」
「没有，亲爱的姑妈，他没提回来。只写了几行，说一桩正经事。」
「哦，是了，正是，」乔利夫小姐干巴巴地说，为侄女这份似乎不信任她的态度，觉得有点受伤。
乔利夫小姐准会说，安娜斯塔西娅的心思她摸得一清二楚，什么秘密也瞒不过她。安娜斯塔西娅准会说，姑妈什么都晓得，只除了几件小小的秘密；而事实上，这一个对那一个的了解，大约也就到年长者对年轻人所宜知道的程度。「心自有它的疆域，它能自成地狱的天堂、天堂的地狱。」人间一切慰藉里，这是最大的一种：心自有它的疆域。心是一座攻不破的堡垒，凭它可拒一切来犯者；心是一处圣所，昼夜向被追赶的人敞开；心是一座百花的园囿，纵在夏天的旱季，浓荫也送人清凉。我们想把迷宫的线团交给某个信得过的朋友，可那丝线球太短，除我们自己，谁也引不到全程。有阳光的山顶，有无辜的绿凉棚，有香气过分浓腻的花圃，有湿冷的绝望的地牢，有夜一般黑的、罪孽的*菌蕈*（mycethmi），我们独自走去，谁也不能携手同往。
尤菲米娅·乔利夫小姐本想对韦斯特雷来信这件事干脆不闻不问，也不再置一词；可好奇在女人身上是比自尊更强的势力，好奇又把她引回那封信上。
「谢谢你，亲爱的，谢谢你对我说明。信里若有给我的什么话，你一定会告诉我的。」
「没有，我看一点给我的话也没有，」安娜斯塔西娅说。「回头我拿来给你，他把说的话都给你看；」说着她离了屋子，像是要去取信。这只是个脱身之计，因为她一路觉着韦斯特雷的信在衣袋里烧出一个洞；可她急于不让姑妈看见这封信，直到给它的回信已经付邮；她指望一旦逃出这间屋子，这件事就会从记忆里掉出去。

===Original===

Miss Joliffe fired a parting shot to try to bring her niece to her bearings as she was going out:
“I do not know, my dear, that I should encourage any correspondence from Mr. Westray, if I were you. It would be more seemly, perhaps, that he should write to me on any little matter of business than to you.” But Anastasia feigned not to hear her, and held on her course.
She betook herself to the room that had once been Mr. Sharnall’s, but was now distressingly empty and forlorn, and there finding writing materials, sat down to compose an answer to Westray’s letter. She knew its contents thoroughly well, she knew its expressions almost by heart, yet she spread it out on the table before her, and read and reread it as many times as if it were the most difficult of cryptograms.
“Dearest Anastasia,” it began, and she found a grievance in the very first word, “Dearest.” What right had he to call her “Dearest”? She was one of those unintelligible females who do not shower superlatives on every chance acquaintance. She must, no doubt, have been callous as judged by modern standards, or at least, singularly unimaginative, for among her few correspondents she had not one whom she addressed as “dearest.” No, not even her aunt, for at such rare times of absence from home as she had occasion to write to Miss Joliffe, “My dear Aunt Euphemia” was the invocation.
It was curious that this same word “Dearest” had occasioned Westray also considerable thought and dubiety. Should he call her “Dearest Anastasia,” or “Dear Miss Joliffe”? The first sounded too forward, the second too formal. He had discussed this and other details with his mother, and the die had at last fallen on “Dearest.” At the worst such an address could only be criticised as proleptic, since it must be justified almost immediately by Anastasia’s acceptance of his proposal.

> Dearest Anastasia﻿—for dearest you are and ever will be to me﻿—I feel sure that your heart will go out to meet my heart in what I am saying; that your kindness will support me in the important step which has now to be taken.

Anastasia shook her head, though there was no one to see her. There was a suggestion of fate overbearing prudence in Westray’s words, a suggestion that he needed sympathy in an unpleasant predicament, that jarred on her intolerably.

===Chinese===

乔利夫小姐趁她出门，放了临别一枪，想把侄女引回航向：
「亲爱的，我要是你，就不会鼓励韦斯特雷先生同你通信。他若有什么小小正事，写信给我，比写给你合宜些。」可安娜斯塔西娅装作没听见，径自去了。
她进了从前沙纳尔先生的房间——如今空得凄凉冷落——在那里寻着纸笔，坐下来写韦斯特雷那封信的回信。信的内容她早已烂熟，那些措辞几乎背得出；可她还是把它摊在面前桌上，一遍又一遍地读，仿佛它是最难解的密码。
「最亲爱的安娜斯塔西娅」，信是这样起头的，而头一个词她就挑出了委屈：「最亲爱的」。他有什么权利叫她「最亲爱的」？她是那种不可解的女人，不肯把最高级的形容词乱洒给泛泛之交。以现代的标准看，她无疑该算无情，至少也惊人地缺乏想象力：她通信的人寥寥无几，其中没有一个她称作「最亲爱的」。不，连姑妈也不是；她偶尔离家、需要给乔利夫小姐写信的时候，抬头写的是「亲爱的尤菲米娅姑妈」。
说来奇怪，正是「最亲爱的」这个词，也叫韦斯特雷大费斟酌、大犯踌躇。称「最亲爱的安娜斯塔西娅」呢，还是「亲爱的乔利夫小姐」？头一个显得太冒进，第二个又太板正。他同母亲商量过这一点和别的细节，骰子终于落在了「最亲爱的」上。往最坏处说，这样的称呼也只能被批评为提前量——反正安娜斯塔西娅一接受他的求婚，它立刻就名正言顺了。

> 最亲爱的安娜斯塔西娅——因为最亲爱的你于我正是、也永远是——我深信，你的心会迎着我所说的话前来会我；你的好意会支持我走这眼下必须走出的一步。

安娜斯塔西娅摇了摇头，虽然并没有人看她。韦斯特雷这些话里有一种命运压倒审慎的意味，一种他在难堪处境里需要同情的意味，叫她听来刺耳极了。

===Original===

> I have known you now a year, and know that my happiness is centred in you; you too have known me a year, and I trust that I have read aright the message that your eyes have been sending to me.

Anastasia found space in the press of her annoyance to laugh. It was more than a smile, it was a laugh, a quiet little laugh to herself, which in a man would have been called a buckle. Her eyes were not hazel brown, they were no brown at all; but then brown rhymed with town, and after all the verse might perhaps be a quotation, and must so be taken only to apply to the situation in general. She read the sentence again, “I have known you now a year; you too have known me a year.” Westray had thought this poetic insistence gave a touch of romance, and balanced the sentence; but to Anastasia it seemed the reiteration of a platitude. If he had known her a year, then she had known him a year, and to a female mind the sequitur was complete.

> Have I read the message right, dearest? Is your heart my own?

Message? What message did he speak of? What message did he imagine she had wished to give *him* with her eyes? He had stared at her persistently for weeks past, and if her eyes sometimes caught his, that was only because she could not help it; except when between whiles she glanced at him of set purpose, because it amused her to see how silly a man in love may look.

> Say that it is; tell me that your heart is my own (and the request seemed to her too preposterous to admit even of comment).
> I watch your present, dear Anastasia, with solicitude. Sometimes I think that you are even now exposed to dangers of whose very existence you know nothing; and sometimes I look forward with anxiety to the future, so undecipherable, if misfortune or death should overtake your aunt. Let me help you to decipher this riddle. Let me be your shield now, and your support in the days to come. Be my wife, and give me the right to be your protector. I am detained in London by business for some days more; but I shall await your answer here with overwhelming eagerness, yet, may I say it? not without hope.
> Your most loving and devoted

**Edward Westray**.

===Chinese===

> 我认识你如今已有一年，知道我的幸福全系于你；你也认识我一年了，我相信，你的眼睛不断向我传来的讯息，我读得没有错。

安娜斯塔西娅在恼怒的重压里还腾出空隙笑了一声。不止是微笑，是笑出声来，静静的一声小笑——若在一个男人身上，就该叫一声嗤笑了。她的眼睛并不是榛棕色，压根儿什么棕色也不是；可 brown（棕）偏同 town（镇）押韵，况且这几句诗兴许还是引文，那就只能算泛泛地切合场面。她把这句又读了一遍：「我认识你如今已有一年；你也认识我一年。」韦斯特雷原以为这诗意的复沓添一分浪漫，句子又对仗工稳；在安娜斯塔西娅看来，却是陈言的重复。他既认识她一年，那她自然也认识他一年——在女性的头脑里，这个推论完整无缺。

> 我读对了那讯息么，最亲爱的？你的心是我的么？

讯息？他说的是什么讯息？他以为她有意用眼睛传给*他*什么讯息？几个星期来他死死盯着她，她的眼睛若有时撞上他的，那也无非因为她躲不开；除非偶尔她故意瞟他一眼，因为看一个恋爱中的男人会傻成什么样子，实在有趣。

> 你说是的吧；告诉我你的心是我的（这个要求在她看来荒唐得连评论都不值一评）。
> 我怀着关切注视着你眼前的处境，亲爱的安娜斯塔西娅。有时我想，你此刻正暴露在你连其存在都一无所知的危险里；有时我又焦虑地展望将来——倘若不幸或死亡临到你姑妈头上，那将来是这样难以索解。让我帮你解这个谜。让我现在做你的盾，将来做你的依靠。做我的妻子，给我做你保护人的权利。我在伦敦被事务绊住还有几天；但我会怀着压倒一切的急切在这里等你的回音——然而，容我说么？——并非没有希望。
> 你最亲爱的、最深情的

**爱德华·韦斯特雷**

===Original===

She folded the letter up with much deliberation, and put it back into its envelope. If Westray had sought far and wide for means of damaging his own cause, he could scarcely have found anything better calculated for that purpose than these last paragraphs. They took away much of that desire to spare, to make unpleasantness as little unpleasant as may be, which generally accompanies a refusal. His sententiousness was unbearable. What right had he to advise before he knew whether she would listen to him? What were these dangers to which she was even now exposed, and from which Mr. Westray was to shield her? She asked herself the question formally, though she knew the answer all the while. Her own heart had told her enough of late, to remove all difficulty in reading between Mr. Westray’s lines. A jealous man is, if possible, more contemptible than a jealous woman. Man’s greater strength postulates a broader mind and wider outlook; and if he fail in these, his failure is more conspicuous than woman’s. Anastasia had traced to jealousy the origin of Westray’s enigmatic remarks; but if she was strong enough to hold him ridiculous for his pains, she was also weak enough to take a woman’s pleasure in having excited the interest of the man she ridiculed.
She laughed again at the proposal that she should join him in deciphering any riddles, still more such as were undecipherable; and the air of patronage involved in his anxiety to provide for her future was the more distasteful in that she had great ideas of providing for it herself. She had told herself a hundred times that it was only affection for her aunt that kept her at home. Were “anything to happen” to Miss Joliffe, she would at once seek her own living. She had often reckoned up the accomplishments which would aid her in such an endeavour. She had received her education﻿—even if it were somewhat desultory and discontinuous﻿—at good schools. She had always been a voracious reader, and possessed an extensive knowledge of English literature, particularly of the masters of fiction; she could play the piano and the violin tolerably, though Mr. Sharnall would have qualified her estimate.

===Chinese===

她从容不迫折好信，放回信封。倘若韦斯特雷遍天下寻访败坏自己大事的法子，也难找到比这最后几段更对路的东西。它们磨掉了她心里那份姑惜之意——拒绝时常有的、把不快减到最轻的那份姑惜。他那副说教腔叫人不能容忍。他还不知道她肯不肯听，就有什么权利先劝上了？她此刻正暴露于其中的、要韦斯特雷先生替她挡住的，又是些什么危险？她一本正经地问自己，尽管答案她早已知道。近来她自己的心告诉她的已经够多，读韦斯特雷先生的字里行间毫无困难。善妒的男人，倘若可以比较，比善妒的女人更可鄙。男人更大的气力，本该配上更宽的心胸、更远的眼界；他若在这两样上失了分，他的失败就比女人的更显眼。安娜斯塔西娅已把韦斯特雷那些没头没脑的话追到嫉妒的根上；可她一面够刚强，能把他这份辛苦看作可笑，一面又够软弱，偏能享受一份女人的快意——她嘲笑的这个男人，毕竟为她动了心。
她想到要同他一道去解什么谜语、尤其是那类根本解不开的谜语，又笑了一声；他急于为她将来打算的那种恩主气派尤其倒她的胃口，因为她对自谋将来怀有宏图。她对自己说过一百遍：留在家里只为着爱姑妈。乔利夫小姐若「有个三长两短」，她立刻自谋生计。她常清点自己的才艺：她受过的教育——纵然断断续续、不成系统——是在好学校里得的。她一向贪读，英国文学知识广博，小说名家尤其烂熟；钢琴和小提琴她弹得、拉得都还过得去——虽然沙纳尔先生未必认可她的自估。

===Original===

She had an easy touch in oils and watercolour, which her father said she must have inherited from his mother﻿—from that Sophia Joliffe who painted the great picture of the flowers and caterpillar, and her spirited caricatures had afforded much merriment to her schoolfellows. She made her own clothes, and was sure that she had a taste in matters of dress design and manufacture that would bring her distinction if she were only given the opportunity of employing it; she believed that she had an affection for children, and a natural talent for training them, though she never saw any at Cullerne. With gifts such as these, which must be patent to others as well as herself, there would surely be no difficulty in obtaining an excellent place as governess if she should ever determine to adopt that walk of life; and she was sometimes inclined to gird at Fate, which for the present led her to deprive the world of these benefits.
In her inmost heart, however, she doubted whether she would be really justified in devoting herself to teaching; for she was conscious that she might be called to fill a higher mission, and to instruct by the pen rather than by word of mouth. As every soldier carries in his knapsack the baton of the Field Marshal, so every girl in her teens knows that there lie hidden in the recesses of her armoire, the robes and coronet and full insignia of a first-rate novelist. She may not choose to take them out and air them, the crown may tarnish by disuse, the moth of indolence may corrupt, but there lies the panoply in which she may on any day appear fully dight, for the astonishment of an awakening world. Jane Austen and Maria Edgworth are heroines, whose aureoles shine in the painted windows of such airy castles; Charlotte Brontë wrote her masterpieces in a seclusion as deep as that of Bellevue Lodge; and Anastasia Joliffe thought many a time of that day when, afar off from her watchtower in quiet Cullerne, she would follow the triumphant progress of an epoch-making romance.
It would be published under a nom de plume, of course, she would not use her own name till she had felt her feet; and the choice of the pseudonym was the only definite step towards this venture that she had yet made. The period was still uncertain.

===Chinese===

油画水彩她下笔轻松，父亲说这必是承自他母亲——就是画那幅花与毛虫大画的索菲娅·乔利夫——她那笔泼辣的漫画，当年给同学们添了许多乐子。她自己的衣裳自己做，而且确信自己在服装设计与剪裁上的品味，只要给她施展的机会，必能出人头地；她相信自己爱孩子、有管教孩子的天分——虽然她在卡勒恩从没见过一个孩子。凭着这样一些天资——别人同她自己一样看得一清二楚——倘使她当真决意走那条路，谋一个上好的家庭教师位置必无难处；有时她简直想埋怨命运，怪它暂且引着她，叫世界平白错失这些好处。
然而在她心底，她又疑心献身教学是否真算名正言顺；因为她自觉或许蒙召担一个更高的使命：用笔教化世人，而非口传。正如每个士兵的行囊里都背着元帅的权杖，每个少女都知道，她衣柜的深处藏着一流小说家的全套袍服、冠冕和徽记。她未必肯取出它们来晾一晾；冠冕会因久置而失色，懒惰的蠹鱼会蛀蚀它们；可全副披挂就躺在那里，随便哪一天她都可以披挂齐全，去叫一个惊醒的世界瞠目。简·奥斯汀和玛丽亚·埃奇沃思是圣女，她们的光环镶在这类空中楼阁的彩绘窗上；夏洛蒂·勃朗特在深居中写出杰作，那深居同美景小筑不相上下；安娜斯塔西娅·乔利夫多少次想过那一天：她从安静的卡勒恩这瞭望塔上远远望见，一部开时代的罗曼司正奏着凯歌行进。
书当然要用笔名出版；不摸清脚下的路，她不会用真名；笔名的挑选，是她朝这项事业迄今迈出的唯一确切的一步。时代背景还悬而未决。

===Original===

Sometimes the action was to be placed in the eighteenth century, with tall silver urns and spindled-legged tables, and breast-waisted dresses; sometimes in the struggle of the Roses, when barons swam rivers in full armour after a bloody bout; sometimes in the Civil War, when Vandyke drew the arched eyebrow and taper hand, and when the shadow of death was over all.
It was to the Civil War that her fancy turned oftenest, and now and again, as she sat before her looking-glass, she fancied that she had a Vandyke face herself. And so it was indeed; and if the mirror was fogged and dull and outworn, and if the dress that it reflected was not of plum or amber velvet, one still might fancy that she was a loyalist daughter whose fortunes were fallen with her master’s. The Limner of the King would have rejoiced to paint the sweet, young, oval face and little mouth; he would have found the space between the eyebrow and the eyelid to his liking.
If the plot were still shadowy, her characters were always with her, in armour or sprigged prints; and, the mind being its own place, she took about a little court of her own, where dreadful tragedies were enacted, and valorous deeds done; where passionate young love suffered and wept, and where a mere girl of eighteen, by consummate resolution, daring, beauty, genius, and physical strength, always righted the situation, and brought peace at the last.
With resources such as these, the future did not present itself in dark colours to Anastasia; nor did its riddle appear to her nearly so undecipherable as Mr. Westray had supposed. She would have resented, with all the confidence of inexperience, *any* attempt to furnish her with prospects; and she resented Westray’s offer all the more vigorously because it seemed to carry with it a suggestion of her own forlorn position, to insist unduly on her own good fortune in receiving such a proposal, and on his condescension in making it.
There are women who put marriage in the forefront of life, whose thoughts revolve constantly about it as a centre, and with whom an advantageous match, or, failing that, a match of some sort, is the primary object. There are others who regard marriage as an eventuality, to be contemplated without either eagerness or avoidance, to be accepted or declined according as its circumstances may be favourable or unfavourable.

===Chinese===

有时故事要安在十八世纪，配高高的银壶和细腿桌，配高腰的裙；有时安在玫瑰战争，男爵们一场血战之后，穿着全副甲胄泳过河；有时安在内战，凡·戴克（Vandyke）勾出弓起的眉和尖细的手，死亡的阴影罩着一切。
她的幻想最常回到内战；她坐在镜前，时不时觉得自己正有一张凡·戴克式的脸。也真有一点：哪怕镜子昏昏沉沉、老旧失光，哪怕它照出的衣裙不是梅子色或琥珀色的丝绒，你仍旧不妨想象她是位忠臣之女，气运随故主一同沉沦。王家画师见了这张甜嫩的鹅蛋脸和小小的嘴，也会欣然命笔；他会喜欢那眉与眼睑之间的距离。
情节纵然还朦胧，人物却始终伴着她，或披甲胄，或着碎花布；心既自有疆域，她便随身携着一座小小的宫廷，那里上演惨烈的悲剧，成就英勇的伟业；那里炽热的少年爱情受难、饮泣；那里一个十八岁的弱女，凭着过人的决断、胆色、美貌、天才和膂力，总能扭转乾坤，终底于成。
凭着这样一些本钱，前途在安娜斯塔西娅眼里并不呈暗色；那谜语也远不像韦斯特雷先生设想的那么难解。她会以毫无阅历的人特有的全部自信，*一律*拒斥任何替她安排前程的企图；她拒斥韦斯特雷的提议尤其用力，因为它似乎夹带着她自身处境凄凉的暗示，过分强调她得此求婚是何等福气，又见他屈尊俯就的姿态。
有的女人把婚姻摆在人生的第一位，心思绕着它转，以它为轴；对她们，一门有利的亲事——退一步，随便一门亲事——是首要目标。另一些女人把婚姻看作一件或然之事，既不热望也不回避，视情势顺逆而接受或谢绝。

===Original===

Again, there are some who seem, even from youth, to resolutely eliminate wedlock from their thoughts, to permit themselves no mental discussion upon this subject. Though a man profess that he will never marry, experience has shown that his resolve is often subject to reconsideration. But with unmarrying women the case is different, and unmarried for the most part they remain, for man is often so weak-kneed a creature in matters of the heart, that he refrains from pursuing where an unsympathetic attitude discourages pursuit. It may be that some of these women, also, would wish to reconsider their verdict, but find that they have reached an age when there is no place for repentance; yet, for the most part, woman’s resolve upon such matters is more stable than man’s, and that because the interests at stake in marriage are for her more vital than can ever be the case with man.
It was to the class of indifferentists that Anastasia belonged; she neither sought nor shunned a change of state, but regarded marriage as an accident that, in befalling her, might substantially change the outlook. It would render a life of teaching, no doubt, impossible; domestic or maternal cares might to some extent trammel even literary activity (for, married or not married, she was determined to fulfil her mission of writing), but in no case was she inclined to regard marriage as an escape from difficulties, as the solution of so trivial a problem as that of existence.
She read Westray’s letter once more from beginning to end. It was duller than ever. It reflected its writer; she had always thought him unromantic, and now he seemed to her intolerably prosaic, conceited, pettifogging, utilitarian. To be his wife! She had rather slave as a nursery-governess all her life! And how could she write fiction with such a one for mentor and company? He would expect her to be methodic, to see that eggs were fresh, and beds well aired. So, by thinking, she reasoned herself into such a theoretic reprobation of this attempt upon her, that his offer became a heinous crime. If she answered him shortly, brusquely, nay rudely, it would be but what he deserved for making her ridiculous to herself by so absurd a proposal, and she opened her writing-case with much firmness and resolution.
It was a little wooden case covered in imitation leather, with *Papeterie* stamped in gold upon the top.

===Chinese===

还有一些女人，仿佛从少女时代起就把婚姻毅然逐出思绪，不容自己在这个题目上作任何心内的讨论。男人虽宣称终身不娶，经验表明那决心往往还有复议的余地。可在抱定不嫁的女人身上，情形便不同了；她们多半就真的不嫁，因为男人在情场常常是那样一个软膝头的动物，对方既以冷面挫折追求，他也就歇手了。这些女人里，也许有的也想复议她们的判决，却发现已到了无可悔改的年纪；但大体而论，女人在这类事上的决心比男人的稳固——因为婚姻里押上的利害，对她是性命攸关，男人永不能及。
安娜斯塔西娅正属于冷淡派这一类；她既不追求也不回避身份的变更，只把婚姻看作一场变故，倘或落到头上，前景必大为改观。它无疑将使教书的生涯不可能；家累或儿女之累，多少也要牵制文学的事业（因为嫁人不嫁人，她都拿定主意要完成写作的使命）；可无论怎样，她都不肯把婚姻看作脱困的门径，看作解决「生计」这样一桩区区问题的答案。
她把韦斯特雷的信从头到尾又读了一遍。比先前更乏味了。信如其人；她一向觉得他不浪漫，如今更觉得他乏味得不可容忍、自负、琐屑、实惠主义。做他的妻子！她宁愿终身做保姆家庭教师苦熬！同这样一个人做良师、做伴侣，她还怎么写小说？他要她事事有条有理，要她查点鸡蛋的新鲜、床褥的晾晒。这样左思右想，她竟从道理上把这次求婚骂成了一桩滔天罪状。倘若她回信回得简慢、生硬、甚至无礼，那也只是他应得的报应——谁叫他提出这样荒唐的求婚，叫她自己在自己眼里成了笑柄。于是她以十足的坚定果决，打开了文具匣。
那是一只小木匣，包着充皮纸，顶上烫金印着 *Papeterie*（文具匣）的字样。

===Original===

She had no exaggerated notions as to its intrinsic worth, but it was valuable in her eyes as being a present from her father. It was, in fact, the only gift he ever had bestowed upon her; but on this he had expended at least half a crown, in a fit of unusual generosity when he sent her with a great flourish of trumpets to Mrs. Howard’s school at Carisbury. She remembered his very words. “Take this, child,” he said; “you are now going to a first-class place of education, and it is right that you should have a proper equipment,” and so gave her the *papeterie*. It had to cover a multitude of deficiencies, and poor Anastasia lamented that it had not been a new hairbrush, half a dozen pocket-handkerchiefs, or even a sound pair of shoes.
Still it had stood in good stead, for with it she had written all her letters ever since, and being the only receptacle with lock and key to which she had access, she had made it a little ark and coffer for certain girlish treasures. With such it was stuffed so full that they came crowding out as she opened it. There were several letters to which romance attached, relics of that delightful but far too short school-time at Carisbury; there was her programme, with rudely-scribbled names of partners, for the splendid dance at the term’s end, to which a selection of other girls’ brothers were invited; a pressed rose given her by someone which she had worn in her bosom on that historic occasion, and many other equally priceless mementoes. Somehow these things seemed now neither so romantic nor so precious as on former occasions; she was even inclined to smile, and to make light of them, and then a little bit of paper fluttered off the table on to the floor. She stooped and picked up the flap of an envelope with the coronet and “Fording” stamped in black upon it which she had found one day when Westray’s wastepaper basket was emptied. It was a simple device enough, but it must have furnished her food for thought, for it lay under her eyes on the table for at least ten minutes before she put it carefully back into the *papeterie*, and began her letter to Westray.
She found no difficulty in answering, but the interval of reflection had soothed her irritation, and blunted her animosity.

===Chinese===

它本身值多少钱，她没有夸张的念头；可它是父亲给的，在她眼里就贵重。事实上，那是父亲唯一送过她的一件礼物；为了它，他还破费了至少半克朗——那是他一时罕见的慷慨，风风光光把她送进卡利斯伯里的霍华德太太（Mrs. Howard）学校的时候。她记得他的原话。「拿着，孩子，」他说；「你如今进的是一流学堂，理当有一套像样的行头。」于是把这文具匣给了她。它得遮掩多少短缺，可怜的安娜斯塔西娅叹只叹它不是一把新发刷、半打手帕、或者一双结实的鞋。
话虽如此，它也着实顶了用：打那以后她所有的信都是用它写的；又因为这是她能沾手的唯一带锁的家什，她把它当作小小方舟和宝匣，收着几件少女的珍藏。匣子塞得满满的，一开盖，那些东西便拥挤着往外跳。有几封信带着罗曼史的光晕，是卡利斯伯里那段快活却太短的学堂岁月的遗物；有她学期末那场盛大舞会的节目单，舞伴的名字涂得歪歪扭扭——那晚还请了几位别家女孩的兄弟；有一朵别人送的玫瑰，她在那个历史性的场合戴在胸前；还有许多同样无价的纪念物。不知怎么，这些东西如今显得既不那么浪漫、也不那么贵重了；她甚至想笑，想拿它们打趣；这时一小片纸从桌上飘落在地。她俯身拾起——是一片信封的封盖，黑字压着一顶冠冕和「福丁」（Fording）的字样，是她有一天在韦斯特雷字纸篓倒空时捡到的。这花样其实再简单不过，可它必是喂了她不少思量：它在桌上她眼前躺了足足十分钟，她才小心把它收回文具匣，动笔给韦斯特雷写信。
回信她并不觉得难；只是这一段沉思缓了她的火气，钝了她的敌意。

===Original===

Her reply was neither brusque nor rude, it leant rather to conventionalism than to originality, and she used, after all, those phrases which have been commonplaces in such circumstances, since man first asked and woman first refused. She thanked Mr. Westray for the kind interest which he had taken in her, she was deeply conscious of the consideration which he had shown her. She was grieved﻿—sincerely grieved﻿—to tell him that things could not be as he wished. She was so afraid that her letter would seem unkind; she did not mean it to be unkind. However difficult it was to say it now, she thought it was the truest kindness not to disguise from him that things *never* could be as he wished. She paused a little to review this last sentiment, but she allowed it to remain, for she was anxious to avoid any recrudescence of the suppliant’s passion, and to show that her decision was final. She should always feel the greatest esteem for Mr. Westray; she trusted that the present circumstances would not interrupt their friendship in any way. She hoped that their relations might continue as in the past, and in this hope she remained very truly his.
She gave a sigh of relief when the letter was finished, and read it through carefully, putting in commas and semicolons and colons at what she thought appropriate places. Such punctilio pleased her; it was, she considered, due from one who aspired to a literary style, and aimed at making a living by the pen. Though this was the first answer to a proposal that she had written on her own account, she was not altogether without practice in such matters, as she had composed others for her heroines who had found themselves in like position. Her manner, also, was perhaps unconsciously influenced by a perusal of *The Young Person’s Compleat Correspondent, and Guide to Answers to Be Given in the Various Circumstances of Life*, which, in a tattered calf covering, formed an item in Miss Euphemia’s library.
It was not till the missive was duly sealed up and posted that she told her aunt of what had happened. “There is Mr. Westray’s letter,” she said, “if you would care to read it,” and passed over to Miss Joliffe the piece of white paper on which a man had staked his fate.

===Chinese===

她的回信既不生硬也不无礼，倒偏于因袭套语而非独出心裁；她用的终究是那一类措辞——自男人第一次开口、女人第一次回绝以来，这种场合的陈言。她感谢韦斯特雷先生对她怀的好意；她深深领受他待她的体贴。她难过——真诚地难过——告诉他，事情不能如他所愿。她很怕这封信显得无情；她并不想叫它无情。无论此刻说出口多么难，她认为对他不加掩饰地说明事情*永远*不能如他所愿，才是最真的好意。这一句她略停了停，推敲一番，终究留下了；因为她急于避免求婚人的热情死灰复燃，急于表明她的决定是最后的。她对韦斯特雷先生将永远怀有最大的敬意；她相信眼下的情形绝不会妨害两人的友谊。她希望两人的交往照旧继续下去；怀着这个希望，她依旧是他真诚的——
信写完，她舒了一大口气，仔细通读一遍，在自以为相宜的地方添上逗号、分号、冒号。这样的谨严使她愉快；她认为，凡志于文体、想以笔墨谋生的人，理当如此。这虽是她头一回替自己写拒婚信，她在这类事上却并非毫无练习——她替她笔下处于同样境地的女主角们拟过好几封。她的笔调，兴许也无意识地受了那本《青年书信大全——人生种种情境应对指南》（*The Young Person's Compleat Correspondent*）的影响；那本书犊皮封面已破，是尤菲米娅小姐藏书里的一部。
直到信笺妥妥帖帖封好付邮，她才把事情的始末告诉姑妈。「这是韦斯特雷先生的信，」她说，「您要是有兴致看看；」说着把那张白纸递给乔利夫小姐——一个男人在那上面押下了他的命运。

===Original===

Miss Joliffe took the letter with an attempt to assume an indifferent manner, which was unsuccessful, because an offer of marriage has about it a certain exhalation and atmosphere that betrays its importance even to the most unsuspicious. She was a slow reader, and, after wiping and adjusting her spectacles, sat down for a steady and patient consideration of the matter before her.
But the first word that she deciphered, “Dearest,” startled her composure, and she pressed on through the letter with a haste that was foreign to her disposition. Her mouth grew rounder as she read, and she sighed out “Dear’s” and “Dear Anastasia’s” and “Dear Child’s” at intervals as a relief to her feelings.
Anastasia stood by her, following the lines of writing that she knew by heart, with all the impatience of one who is reading ten times faster than another who turns the page.
Miss Joliffe’s mind was filled with conflicting emotions; she was glad at the prospect of a more assured future that was opening before her niece, she was hurt at not having been taken sooner into confidence, for Anastasia must certainly have known that he was going to propose; she was chagrined at not having noticed a courtship which had been carried on under her very eyes; she was troubled at the thought that the marriage would entail the separation from one who was to her as a child.
How weary she would find it to walk alone down the long paths of old age! how hard it was to be deprived of a dear arm on whose support she had reckoned for when “the slow dark hours begin”! But she thrust this reflection away from her as selfish, and contrition for having harboured it found expression in a hand wrinkled and roughened by hard wear, which stole into Anastasia’s.
“My dear,” she said, “I am very glad at your good fortune; this is a great thing that has befallen you.” A general content that Anastasia should have received a proposal silenced her misgivings.
To the recipient, an offer of marriage, be it good, bad, or indifferent, to be accepted or to be refused, brings a certain complacent satisfaction. She may pretend to make light of it, to be displeased at it, to resent it, as did Anastasia; but in her heart of hearts there lurks the self-appreciating reflection that she has won the completest admiration of a man.

===Chinese===

乔利夫小姐接信时竭力摆出一副淡漠的神气，却没有摆成；因为求婚信自有一种气息和氛围，连最不多心的人也瞒不过。她读书慢；擦好眼镜、架正之后，坐下来对她面前的东西作一番沉着耐心的推究。
可她辨认出的头一个词「最亲爱的」就惊了她的镇定，她带着一种与她性情不合的急切往信尾赶去。她一面读，嘴愈张愈圆，又隔三差五叹出一声「亲爱的」「亲爱的安娜斯塔西娅」「亲爱的孩子」，给自己胸口松一松绑。
安娜斯塔西娅立在她旁边，随着那几行她早已背熟的字迹往下走，怀着那种比翻页的人快读十倍的人的全部心焦。
乔利夫小姐心里充满了交战的情感：侄女面前展开一个更有保障的前程，她高兴；她伤心自己没有早被引为心腹——安娜斯塔西娅必定早知道他要求婚；她懊恼一场求婚就在自己眼皮底下进行，自己竟没有察觉；她愁苦这桩婚事意味着同一个于她如同亲女的人别离。
独自一人走下老年那长长的路径，将是怎样疲惫！「当缓慢的黑夜时辰开始」，却失去一支可倚的亲爱的臂膀，何其难堪！可她把这念头当作自私推开了；为曾怀过这念头的悔疚，化做一只因操劳而皱缩粗糙的手，悄悄伸进安娜斯塔西娅的手里。
「亲爱的，」她说，「你的福气叫我真高兴；落在你身上的是一件大事。」安娜斯塔西娅得了一纸求婚这个笼统的满足，压下了她种种疑虑。
对收信人来说，一纸求婚——无论好坏平庸，无论接受回绝——总带来某种自得的满足。她尽可以装作满不在乎、装作恼它、怨它，像安娜斯塔西娅那样；可心底深处总潜伏着那一丝自我欣赏的念头：她赢来了一个男人最完全的倾慕。

===Original===

If he be a man that she would not marry under any conditions, if he be a fool, or a spendthrift, or an evil-liver, he is still a man, and she has captured him. Her relations share in the same pleasurable reflections. If the offer is accepted, then a future has been provided for one whose future, maybe, was not too certain; if it is declined, then they congratulate themselves on the high morale or strong common sense of a kinswoman who refuses to be won by gold, or to link her destiny with an unsuitable partner.
“It is a great thing, my dear, that has befallen you,” Miss Joliffe repeated. “I wish you all happiness, dear Anastasia, and may all blessings wait upon you in this engagement.”
“Aunt,” interrupted her niece, “please don’t say that. I have refused him, *of course*; how could you think that I should marry Mr. Westray? I never have thought of any such thing with him. I never had the least idea of his writing like this.”
“You have refused him?” said the elder lady with a startled emphasis. Again a selfish reflection crossed her mind﻿—they were not to be parted after all﻿—and again she put it resolutely away. She ran over in her mind all the possible objections that could have influenced her niece in arriving at such a conclusion. Religion was the keynote of Miss Joliffe’s life; to religion her thought reverted as the needle to the pole, and to it she turned for an explanation now. It must be some religious consideration that had proved an obstacle to Anastasia.
“I do not think you need find any difficulty in his having been brought up as a Wesleyan,” she said, with a profound conviction that she had put her finger on the matter, and with some consciousness of her own perspicacity. “His father has been dead some time, and though his mother is still alive, you would not have to live with her. I do not think, dear, she would at all wish you to become a Methodist. As for our Mr. Westray, your Mr. Westray, I should say now,” and she assumed that expression of archness which is considered appropriate to such occasions, “I am sure he is a sound Churchman. He goes regularly to the minster on Sundays, and I dare say, being an architect, and often in church on weekdays, he has found out that the order of the Church of England is more satisfactory than that of any other sect.

===Chinese===

哪怕他是她任何条件下都不会嫁的人，是傻瓜、败家子、行止不端的人，他到底是个男人，而她把他俘获了。她的亲眷也分享同一份愉快的感想。婚若受了，一个前途本不牢靠的人就有了着落；婚若回了，她们又庆辛自家这位女眷志气高、常识足，不肯为黄金所动，不肯把命运拴在不相配的人身上。
「落在你身上的是一件大事，亲爱的，」乔利夫小姐又说了一遍。「我祝你事事幸福，亲爱的安娜斯塔西娅，愿一切福分伴随你这订婚。」
「姑妈，」侄女打断她，「请您别说这个。我回绝他了，*当然*；您怎么会想我要嫁韦斯特雷先生？我同他从来没有过半点这样的意思。我一点也没想到他会写这样的信。」
「你回绝他了？」年长的女士愕然加重了语气。又一个自私的念头掠过心头——她们终究不用分离了——又被她毅然推开。她在心里把可能左右侄女得出这个结论的种种理由飞快过了一遍。宗教是乔利夫小姐一生的主音；她的心思一遇疑难便如针之指北，此刻她又向宗教求解。必是某个宗教上的考虑，成了安娜斯塔西娅的障碍。
「我想，他是循道会人家出身，这你不必觉得有什么难处，」她说，深信自己一针见血，也颇有几分自赏机敏。「他父亲去世有些年了；母亲虽还在，你也不必同她住。亲爱的，我看她也决不愿你做循道宗徒。至于我们的韦斯特雷先生——我如今该说*你的*韦斯特雷先生了，」她摆出适合这种场合的那种俏皮神气，「我确信他是国教的中坚。星期天他按时上大教堂；我想，他既是个建筑师，平 日又常在教堂里，必已发现英国国教的仪制比任何别的宗派更令人满意。

===Original===

Though I am sure I do not wish to say one word against Wesleyans; they are no doubt true Protestants, and a bulwark against more serious errors. I rejoice that your lover’s early training will have saved him from any inclination to ritualism.”
“My dear aunt,” Anastasia broke in, with a stress of earnest deprecation on the “dear” that startled her aunt, “please do *not* go on like that. Do not call Mr. Westray my lover; I have told you that I will have nothing to do with him.”
Miss Joliffe’s thoughts had moved through a wide arc. Now that this offer of marriage was about to be refused, now that this engagement was not to be, the advantages that it offered stood out in high relief. It seemed too sad that the curtain should be rung down just as the action of a drama of intense interest was beginning, that the good should slip through their fingers just as they were grasping it. She gave no thought now to that fear of a lonely old age which had troubled her a few minutes before; she only saw the provision for the future which Anastasia was wilfully sacrificing. Her hand tightened automatically, and crumpled a long piece of paper that she was holding. It was only a milkman’s bill, and yet it might perhaps have unconsciously given a materialistic colour to her thoughts.
“We should not reject any good thing that is put before us,” she said a little stiffly, “without being very certain that we are right to do so. I do not know what would become of you, Anastasia, if anything were to happen to me.”
“That is exactly what he says, that is the very argument which he uses. Why should you take such a gloomy view of things? Why should something *happening* always mean something bad? Let us hope something good will happen, that someone else will make me a better offer.” She laughed, and went on reflectively: “I wonder whether Mr. Westray will come back here to lodge; I hope he won’t.”
Hardly were the words out of her mouth when she was sorry for uttering them, for she saw the look of sadness which overspread Miss Joliffe’s face.
“Dear aunt,” she cried, “I am so sorry; I didn’t mean to say that. I know what a difference it would make; we cannot afford to lose our last lodger. I hope he *will* come back, and I will do everything I can to make things comfortable, short of marrying him. I will earn some money myself. I will *write*.”
“How will you write?

===Chinese===

虽然我决无半句要说循道会坏话的意思；他们无疑是真正的新教徒，是抵御更严重谬误的一道壁垒。你的情人幼年的教养使他免于沾染仪式主义的倾向，我为此称谢。」
「亲爱的姑妈，」安娜斯塔西娅插进来，那个「亲爱的」说得恳切极了，把姑妈吓了一跳，「请*千万*别往下说了。别叫韦斯特雷先生我的情人；我告诉过您，我同他没有任何相干。」
乔利夫小姐的思绪已转过一个大大的弧。这桩婚事既然要遭回绝，这订婚既然成不了，它所能给的好处便赫然凸现出来。大幕偏在一出紧张好戏开场之际落下，好事偏在伸手正要及时溜走，未免太可惜。此刻她全忘了几分钟前还烦扰她的孤老之忧；她只看见安娜斯塔西娅任性牺牲掉的前程保障。她的手不由自主收紧，把捏着的一长条纸揉皱了。那不过是一张送奶人的账单，可它或许已不知不觉给她的思绪添了一层唯物主义的颜色。
「摆在面前的好处，我们不该随手推掉，」她略有些生硬地说，「除非十分拿得准我们推得对。安娜斯塔西娅，倘若我有个三长两短，真不知你会怎样。」
「他说的正是这句话，他用的正是这个理。您为什么把事情看得这样阴？为什么『出事』就总是出坏事？我们不如盼着出点好事，盼着另有人向我提一门更好的亲。」她笑了，又若有所思地接着说：「不知韦斯特雷先生还回不回来住；我盼望他不回来。」
话音未落她就后悔了，因为她看见一层悲色漫上乔利夫小姐的脸。
「亲爱的姑妈，」她喊道，「真对不起；我不是有意那样说的。我知道这关系多大；咱们再赔不起最后一个房客。我盼望他*回来*，我也要尽力把一切弄得舒舒服服——只是不嫁他。我自己要去挣钱。我要*写信*。」
「你要怎样写信？

===Original===

Who is there to write to?” Miss Joliffe said, and then the blank look on her face grew blanker, and she took out her handkerchief. “There is no one to help us. Anyone who ever cared for us is dead long ago; there is no one to write to now.”

===Chinese===

写信给谁呢？」乔利夫小姐说；随后她脸上茫然的神色更茫然了，掏出手绢来。「没有人帮我们。但凡爱过我们的人，早就死光了；如今没有一个人可写信了。」

## XVII / 十七
===Original===

Westray played the role of rejected lover most conscientiously; he treated the episode of his refusal on strictly conventional lines. He assured himself and his mother that the light of his life was extinguished, that he was the most unhappy of mortals. It was at this time that he wrote some verses called “Autumn,” with a refrain of﻿—

> “For all my hopes are cold and dead,

And fallen like the fallen leaves,”

===Chinese===

韦斯特雷以最尽责的态度扮演了遭拒的恋人；他严格按惯例处理这遭拒的一章。他叫自己和他的母亲都相信，他生命之光熄灭了，他是世人中最不幸的。就是在这个时候，他写了几行题为《秋》的诗，副歌是——

> 「因为我一切的希望都已冰冷死灭，

像落叶一样凋落，」

===Original===

which were published in the *Clapton Methodist*, and afterwards set to music by a young lady who wished to bind up another wounded heart. He attempted to lie awake of nights with indifferent success, and hinted in conversation at the depressing influence which insomnia exerts over its victims. For several meals in succession he refused to eat heartily of such dishes as he did not like, and his mother felt serious anxiety as to his general state of health. She inveighed intemperately against Anastasia for having refused her son, but then she would have inveighed still more intemperately had Anastasia accepted him. She wearied him with the portentous gloom which she affected in his presence, and quoted Lady Clara Vere de Vere’s cruelty in turning honest hearts to gall, till even the rejected one was forced to smile bitterly at so inapposite a parallel.
Though Mrs. Westray senior poured out the vials of her wrath on Anastasia for having refused to become Mrs. Westray junior, she was at heart devoutly glad at the turn events had taken. At heart Westray could not have said whether he was glad or sorry. He told himself that he was deeply in love with Anastasia, and that this love was further ennobled by a chivalrous desire to shield her from evil; but he could not altogether forget that the unfortunate event had at least saved him from the unconventionality of marrying his landlady’s niece. He told himself that his grief was sincere and profound, but it was possible that chagrin and wounded pride were after all his predominant feelings. There were other reflections which he thrust aside as indecorous at this acute stage of the tragedy, but which, nevertheless, were able to exercise a mildly consoling influence in the background. He would be spared the anxieties of early and impecunious marriage, his professional career would not be weighted by family cares, the whole world was once more open before him, and the slate clean. These were considerations which could not prudently be overlooked, though it would be unseemly to emphasise them too strongly when the poignancy of regret should dominate every other feeling.
He wrote to Sir George Farquhar, and obtained ten days’ leave of absence on the score of indisposition; and he wrote to Miss Euphemia Joliffe to tell her that he intended to seek other rooms.

===Chinese===

诗发表在《克拉普顿循道会刊》（*Clapton Methodist*）上，后来又由一位愿意再包扎一颗创伤之心的小姐谱了曲。他尝试夜里失眠，成绩平平；又逢人暗示失眠症对患者的压抑影响。他接连几顿不肯痛快吃那些他本不喜欢的菜，母亲对他总的健康状况着实担忧起来。她痛骂安娜斯塔西娅回绝了她的儿子，骂得毫无节制；可倘若安娜斯塔西娅接受了他，她骂得只会更无节制。她在他面前摆出的一副不祥的愁云惨雾叫他厌倦，又引克拉拉·维尔·德·维尔小姐把诚实的心变成苦胆的残忍为证，直到连遭拒的人也不得不对这样风马牛不相及的比照苦笑一声。
老韦斯特雷太太为了安娜斯塔西娅不肯做小韦斯特雷太太，把满腔怒火一盆盆泼在她身上；心底里却为事情的这一转机虔诚地欢喜。韦斯特雷自己心底里是喜是悲，他也说不上。他对自己说，他深爱着安娜斯塔西娅，这份爱又因一个侠义的愿望——护她免遭邪恶——而更为高贵；可他忘不掉，这件不幸的事至少免了他一桩不合常规的婚事：娶房东太太的侄女。他对自己说，他的悲伤真诚而深切；可懊恼和受伤的自尊究竟是不是他主要的感情，也难说。还有一些念头，在这悲剧的剧烈阶段他斥为不雅、推在一旁，可它们到底在背景里起着轻微的安慰作用。他将免于早年拮据婚姻的种种焦虑；他的职业生涯不致压上家累；整个世界又在他面前敞开，账板擦得干干净净。这些都是不宜轻看的考虑——虽然在悔恨的锥心应当压倒一切之时，过分强调它们未免失态。
他写信给乔治·法夸尔爵士，托病告了十天假；又写信给尤菲米娅·乔利夫小姐，说他打算另寻寓所。

===Original===

From the first he had decided that this latter step was inevitable. He could not bear the daily renewal of regret, the daily opening of the wound that would be caused by the sight of Anastasia, or by such chance intercourse with her as further residence at Bellevue Lodge must entail. There is no need to speculate whether his decision was influenced in part by a concession to humiliated pride; men do not take pleasure in revisiting the scenes of a disastrous rout, and it must be admitted that the possibility of summoning a lost love to his presence when he rang for boiling water, had in it something of the grotesque. He had no difficulty in finding other lodgings by correspondence, and he spared himself the necessity of returning at all to his former abode by writing to ask Clerk Janaway to move his belongings.
One morning, a month later, Miss Joliffe sat in that room which had been occupied by the late Mr. Sharnall. She was alone, for Anastasia had gone to the office of the *Cullerne Advertiser* with an announcement in which one A. J. intimated that she was willing to take a post as nursery-governess. It was a bright morning but cold, and Miss Joliffe drew an old white knitted shawl closer about her, for there was no fire in the grate. There was no fire because she could not afford it, yet the sun pouring in through the windows made the room warmer than the kitchen, where the embers had been allowed to die out since breakfast. She and Anastasia did without fire on these bright autumn days to save coals; they ate a cold dinner, and went early to bed for the same reason, yet the stock in the cellar grew gradually less.
