There was no expression of surprise, curiosity, indignation or alarm; nothing, in fact, except the utmost courtesy, a little more distant perhaps than usual, but not markedly so.
Westray had been unable to conjecture what would be the nature of Lord Blandamer’s answer. He had thought of many possibilities, of the impostor’s flight, of lavish offers of hush-money, of passionate appeals for mercy, of scornful and indignant denial. But in all his imaginings he had never imagined this. Ever since he had sent his own letter, he had been doubtful of its wisdom, and yet he had not been able to think of any other course that he would have preferred. He knew that the step he had taken in warning the criminal was quixotic, and yet it seemed to him that Lord Blandamer had a certain right to see his own family portrait and papers, before they were used against him. He could not feel sorry that he had given the opportunity, though he had certainly hoped that Lord Blandamer would not avail himself of it.
But go to Fording he would not. That, at any rate, no fantastic refinement of fair play could demand of him. He knew his mind at least on this point; he would answer at once, and he got out a sheet of paper for his refusal. It was easy to write the number of his house, and the street, and Cullerne, and the formal “My lord,” which he used again for the address. But what then? What reason was he to give for his refusal?

===Chinese===

他下楼时，那封叫他害怕的信正摆在桌上。他极不情愿拆开它。他几乎奇怪字迹竟还是老样子；仿佛他料着那笔划该发颤，墨水本身该是血红的。布兰达默勋爵致谢韦斯特雷先生的来信。他当然愿意见一见韦斯特雷先生说起的那幅画和那些家族文件；可否请韦斯特雷先生劳驾把画带到福丁来？他抱歉这样有劳；只是福丁画廊里另有一幅画，同新近发现的这幅对照一下，也许有趣。他可以派马车接任何一班火车；韦斯特雷先生无疑会觉得在福丁过夜更方便。
没有惊讶，没有好奇，没有愤慨或惊惶；什么都没有，只有极度的客气——也许比平常略略疏远一点，但不明显。
韦斯特雷一直揣想不到布兰达默勋爵回信的性质。他想过许多可能：冒牌者的远遁、大笔封口钱的提议、动情的乞怜、轻蔑愤慨的否认。可在他一切悬想里，从未悬想到这一种。自寄出那封信起，他就疑心它不明智；可又想不出别的更合他心意的路数。他知道，向罪人通风报信这一步是堂吉诃德式的；可布兰达默勋爵似乎也有几分权利，在自家的画像和文件被用来对付他之前，先看它们一眼。他给了这个机会，并不懊悔；只是他确实巴望过布兰达默勋爵不来利用它。
可去福丁，他是不去的。这一点，再讲究的公平游戏的奇想也要求不到他头上。至少在这件事上他拿得定主意；他要立刻作复，抽出一张纸来写回绝。写门牌、街名、卡勒恩，写抬头那一声「勋爵大人」，都容易。可接下来呢？回绝的理由，他拿什么理由？

===Original===

He could allege no business appointment or other serious engagement as an obstacle, for he himself had said that he was free for a week, and had offered Lord Blandamer to make an appointment on any day. He himself had offered an interview; to draw back now would be mean and paltry in the extreme. It was true that the more he thought of this meeting the more he shrank from it. But it could not be evaded now. It was, after all, only the easiest part of the task that he had set before him, only a prolusion to the tragedy that he would have to play to a finish. Lord Blandamer deserved, no doubt, all the evil that was to fall on him; but in the meanwhile he, Westray, was incapable of refusing this small favour, asked by a man who was entirely at his mercy.
Then he wrote with a shrinking heart, but with yet another fixed purpose, that he would bring the picture to Fording the next day. He preferred not to be met at the station; he would arrive some time during the afternoon, but could only stay an hour at the most, as he had business which would take him on to London the same evening.
It was a fine Autumn day on the morrow, and when the morning mists had cleared away, the sun came out with surprising warmth, and dried the dew on the lawns of many-gardened Cullerne. Towards midday Westray set forth from his lodgings to go to the station, carrying under his arm the picture, lightly packed in lath, and having in his pocket those papers which had fallen out from the frame. He chose a route through back-streets, and walked quickly, but as he passed Quandrill’s, the local maker of guns and fishing-rods, a thought struck him. He stopped and entered the shop.
“Good morning,” he said to the gunsmith, who stood behind the counter; “have you any pistols? I want one small enough to carry in the pocket, but yet something more powerful than a toy.”
Mr. Quandrill took off his spectacles.
“Ah,” he said, tapping the counter with them meditatively. “Let me see. Mr. Westray, is it not, the architect at the minster?”
“Yes,” Westray answered. “I require a pistol for some experiments. It should carry a fairly heavy bullet.”
“Oh, just so,” the man said, with an air of some relief, as Westray’s coolness convinced him that he was not contemplating suicide. “Just so, I see; some experiments.

===Chinese===

他不能托词公务或其他要紧的约会，因为他自己说过这一星期都有空，还主动请布兰达默勋爵随便定哪一天。会面是他自己提的；此刻缩回去，就未免太卑琐。不错，这场会面他越想越怕。可如今已无法回避。这毕竟只是他给自己排下的任务里最容易的一段，只是他不得不到底演完的那出悲剧的序曲。布兰达默勋爵无疑恶贯满盈，该领受将落在他头上的一切；可韦斯特雷呢，一个全然在他掌握之中的人开口求这点小惠，他拒不了。
于是他怀着畏缩的心、却怀着又一个定死的决心，写道：明日他将携画前往福丁。他情愿不必到站相迎；他下午的什么时候到，至多只能停留一个钟头，因为有事当晚要赶去伦敦。
次日是个晴朗的秋日；晨雾散尽，太阳带着惊人的暖意出来，晒干了花园层叠的卡勒恩一片草地上的露水。近午，韦斯特雷从寓所出发去车站，腋下夹着那幅画——薄薄一层板条打的包装——衣袋里揣着从画框里落出的那几件文书。他拣后街的小路走，走得很快；可经过本地造枪造钓竿的匡德里尔（Quandrill）铺子时，一个念头攝住了他。他停步进了店。
「早上好，」他对柜台后的枪匠说；「有手枪吗？我要一只小得能揣在衣袋里的，可又不能是玩物，得有点斤两。」
匡德里尔先生摘下眼镜。
「啊，」他用眼镜笃笃地敲着柜台，沉思地说。「让我想想。是韦斯特雷先生吧，大教堂的建筑师？」
「是，」韦斯特雷答。「我做点实验，要一支手枪。得能打相当重的弹子。」
「哦，正是，」那人说，口气松了些——韦斯特雷的镇定叫他放了心，知道他不是要寻短见。「正是，我明白了；做实验。

===Original===

Well, in that case, I suppose, you would not require any special facilities for loading again quickly, otherwise I should have recommended one of these,” and he took up a weapon from the counter. “They are newfangled things from America, revolving pistols they call them. You can fire them four times running, you see, as quick as you like,” and he snapped the piece to show how well it worked.
Westray handled the pistol, and looked at the barrels.
“Yes,” he said, “that will suit my purpose very well, though it is rather large to carry in the pocket.”
“Oh, you want it for the pocket,” the gunmaker said with renewed surprise in his tone.
“Yes; I told you that already. I may have to carry it about with me. Still, I think this will do. Could you kindly load it for me now?”
“You are sure it’s quite safe,” said the gunmaker.
“I ought to ask *you* that,” Westray rejoined with a smile. “Do you mean it may go off accidentally in my pocket?”
“Oh no, it’s safe enough that way,” said the gunmaker. “It won’t go off unless you pull the trigger.” And he loaded the four barrels, measuring out the powder and shot carefully, and ramming in the wads. “You’ll be wanting more powder and shot than this, I suppose,” he said.
“Very likely,” rejoined the architect, “but I can call for that later.”
He found a heavy country fly waiting for him at Lytchett, the little wayside station which was sometimes used by people going to Fording. It is a seven-mile drive from the station to the house, but he was so occupied in his own reflections, that he was conscious of nothing till the carriage pulled up at the entrance of the park. Here he stopped for a moment while the lodge-keeper was unfastening the bolt, and remembered afterwards that he had noticed the elaborate ironwork, and the nebuly coat which was set over the great gates. He was in the long avenue now, and he wished it had been longer, he wished that it might never end; and then the fly stopped again, and Lord Blandamer on horseback was speaking to him through the carriage window.
There was a second’s pause, while the two men looked each other directly in the eyes, and in that look all doubt on either side was ended. Westray felt as if he had received a staggering blow as he came face to face with naked truth, and Lord Blandamer read Westray’s thoughts, and knew the extent of his discovery.
Lord Blandamer was the first to speak.

===Chinese===

那么，这一来，想必您就不需要特别便于快速再装填的家什了；不然我倒要荐这些当里的一支，」他从柜台上拿起一件武器。「这是美国来的新式玩意儿，他们叫转轮手枪。您瞧，能连放四响，要多快有多快。」他扳动机件，演示它多么利落。
韦斯特雷掂了掂那手枪，看了看枪管。
「好，」他说，「很合我的用途，虽说揣在衣袋里嫌大了一点。」
「哦，您是要揣衣袋的，」枪匠的口气又添了几分惊讶。
「是；我告诉过您了。兴许要随身带着走。不过我想这支就行。麻烦您现在就替我装上好吗？」
「您拿得稳它十分安全？」枪匠说。
「这话*我*倒该问*您*，」韦斯特雷含笑回敬。「您是怕它在我衣袋里自己走火？」
「哦不，那方面尽够安全，」枪匠说。「不扣扳机它不会响。」他量火药、数弹子，装填了四个弹膛，又填进弹塞。「火药和弹子您总还要添些，我猜。」他说。
「多半要，」建筑师答，「不过回头再来拿就是。」
在利切特（Lytchett）——去福丁的人有时用的小路边站——一辆笨重的乡下马车等着他。车站到宅子有七英里车程；他一心沉在自己的思绪里，直到车在园子大门前停住，中间的事一概不知。门房下闩时，他停了片刻，事后还记得自己注意了那精工的铁活和大门上方嵌着的尼布里纹章。此刻他在长长的林道上了；他巴望这条路更长些，巴望它永没有尽头；随后马车又停了，骑在马上的布兰达默勋爵正隔着车窗同他说话。
两个人对望了一秒钟；那一望里，双方的一切疑团都了结了。韦斯特雷觉得自己像当面捱了狠狠一击——同赤裸裸的真相打了照面；布兰达默勋爵也读出了韦斯特雷的心思，知道了这一发现已到了什么地步。
布兰达默勋爵先开口。

===Original===

“I am glad to see you again,” he said with perfect courtesy, “and am very much obliged to you for taking this trouble in bringing the picture.” And he glanced at the crate that Westray was steadying with his hand on the opposite seat. “I only regret that you would not let me send a carriage to Lytchett.”
“Thank you,” said the architect; “on the present occasion I preferred to be entirely independent.” His words were cold, and were meant to be cold, and yet as he looked at the other’s gentle bearing, and the grave face in which sadness was a charm; he felt constrained to abate in part the effect of his own remark, and added somewhat awkwardly: “You see, I was uncertain about the trains.”
“I am riding back across the grass,” Lord Blandamer said, “but shall be at the house before you;” and as he galloped off, Westray knew that he rode exceedingly well.
This meeting, he guessed, had been contrived to avoid the embarrassment of a more formal beginning. It was obvious that their terms of former friendship could no longer be maintained. Nothing would have induced him to have shaken hands, and this Lord Blandamer must have known.
As Westray stepped into the hall through Inigo Jones’ Ionic portico, Lord Blandamer entered from a side-door.
“You must be cold after your long drive. Will you not take a biscuit and a glass of wine?”
Westray motioned away the refreshment which a footman offered him.
“No, thank you,” he said; “I will not take anything.” It was impossible for him to eat or drink in this house, and yet again he softened his words by adding: “I had something to eat on the way.”
The architect’s refusal was not lost upon Lord Blandamer. He had known before he spoke that his offer would not be accepted.
“I am afraid it is useless to ask you to stop the night with us,” he said; and Westray had his rejoinder ready:
“No; I must leave Lytchett by the seven five train. I have ordered the fly to wait.”
He had named the last train available for London, and Lord Blandamer saw that his visitor had so arranged matters, that the interview could not be prolonged for more than an hour.
“Of course, you *could* catch the night-mail at Cullerne Road,” he said. “It is a very long drive, but I sometimes go that way to London myself.”

===Chinese===

「很高兴又见到您，」他彬彬有礼地说，「多谢您费心把画带来。」他瞥了一眼韦斯特雷用手扶在对座上的那件木条箱。「只是遗憾您不肯让我派车去利切特接。」
「谢谢，」建筑师说；「这一回，我情愿完全自己作主。」这话是冷的，也有意要冷；可望着对方温文的态度、那张忧郁自成风致的庄重的脸，他又觉着不得不给自己的话打个折扣，别扭地补了一句：「您瞧，火车时辰我没有把握。」
「我骑马从草地那边回去，」布兰达默勋爵说，「但会先您到家；」他策马驰去，韦斯特雷看得出，他骑得极好。
他猜，这一场相逢是特意安排的，免得正式开场时的尴尬。旧日友谊的那一套名目显然维持不下去了。无论什么都引诱不动他去握手，这一点布兰达默勋爵必也明白。
韦斯特雷跨进伊尼戈·琼斯（Inigo Jones）的爱奥尼亚柱廊、进了大厅时，布兰达默勋爵从一扇边门进来。
「坐了这么久的车，您一定冷了。不吃一块饼干、喝一杯酒吗？」
仆人捧上点心，韦斯特雷摆手挡开。
「不，谢谢，」他说；「我什么都不要。」在这座宅子里他饮食不下；可他又一次把话放软，添了一句：「路上吃过一点了。」
建筑师的回绝，布兰达默勋爵看在眼里。开口之前他就知道这番好意必被推却。
「只怕留您过夜也是白留了，」他说；韦斯特雷的答话早备好了：
「不；我必须赶利切特七点零五分的车。我吩咐了马车等着。」
他说的是去伦敦的末班车；布兰达默勋爵明白了：来客把一切安排得这样，会面拖不过一个钟头。
「当然，您也*可以*在卡勒恩路赶夜班邮车，」他说。「车程很长；不过我自己去伦敦，有时也走那条路。」

===Original===

His words called suddenly to Westray’s recollection that night walk when the station lights of Cullerne Road were seen dimly through the fog, and the stationmaster’s story that Lord Blandamer had travelled by the mail on the night of poor Sharnall’s death. He said nothing, but felt his resolution strengthened.
“The gallery will be the most convenient place, perhaps, to unpack the picture,” Lord Blandamer said; and Westray at once assented, gathering from the other’s manner that this would be a spot where no interruption need be feared.
They went up some wide and shallow stairs, preceded by a footman, who carried the picture.
“You need not wait,” Lord Blandamer said to the man; “we can unpack it ourselves.”
When the wrappings were taken off, they stood the painting on the narrow shelf formed by the top of the wainscot which lined the gallery, and from the canvas the old lord surveyed them with penetrating light-grey eyes, exactly like the eyes of the grandson who stood before him.
Lord Blandamer stepped back a little, and took a long look at the face of this man, who had been the terror of his childhood, who had darkened his middle life, who seemed now to have returned from the grave to ruin him. He knew himself to be in a desperate pass. Here he must make the last stand, for the issue lay between him and Westray. No one else had learned the secret. He understood and relied implicitly on Westray’s fantastic sense of honour. Westray had written that he would “take no steps” till the ensuing Monday, and Lord Blandamer was sure that no one would be told before that day, and that no one had been told yet. If Westray could be silenced all was saved; if Westray spoke, all was lost. If it had been a question of weapons, or of bodily strength, there was no doubt which way the struggle would have ended. Westray knew this well now, and felt heartily ashamed of the pistol that was bulging the breast-pocket on the inside of his coat. If it had been a question of physical attack, he knew now that he would have never been given time, or opportunity for making use of his weapon.

===Chinese===

这话猛然唤起韦斯特雷的记性：那夜的步行，卡勒恩路站台的灯在雾里隐约；站长讲的，可怜的沙纳尔死的那夜，布兰达默勋爵正是坐邮车走的。他没有作声，只觉决心更硬了。
「拆画也许以画廊为最便，」布兰达默勋爵说；韦斯特雷立刻应了，从对方的态度里理会到：那是个不必怕人打搅的地方。
他们由一个仆人捧着画引路，登上宽阔平缓的楼梯。
「不用伺候了，」布兰达默勋爵对那人说；「我们自己拆。」
包装卸去，两人把画竖在画廊护壁板顶上那条窄窄的搁沿上；画布上，老勋爵用一双洞察的浅灰眼睛俯视着他们——同站在他面前的孙子的眼睛，一模一样。
布兰达默勋爵略退后一步，久久端详这张脸：这张脸曾是他童年的恐怖，曾使他的中年蒙上暗影，如今仿佛又从坟墓里回来毁他。他知道自己身在绝地。最后一搏必须在此处作，因为局面只悬在他同韦斯特雷之间。秘密再无第二人知晓。他理解、也全然信赖韦斯特雷那奇想式的荣誉感。韦斯特雷信里写了，在下星期一之前「不采步骤」；布兰达默勋爵确信，那一天之前不会有人知道，此刻也没有人知道。韦斯特雷若能缄默，一切得救；韦斯特雷若开口，一切完蛋。倘若拼的是兵器或膂力，这场角斗的胜负毫无悬念。这一点韦斯特雷如今看得分明，想到自己衣袋里那支把手枪顶得鼓起来的胸前口袋，由衷地羞愧。倘若当真拼的是肉搏，他如今明白，对方根本不会给他拔枪的工夫和机会。

===Original===

Lord Blandamer had travelled north and south, east and west; he had seen and done strange things; he had stood for his life in struggles whence only one could come out alive; but here was no question of flesh and blood﻿—he had to face principles, those very principles on which he relied for respite; he had to face that integrity of Westray which made persuasion or bribery alike impossible. He had never seen this picture before, and he looked at it intently for some minutes; but his attention was all the while concentrated on the man who stood beside him. This was his last chance﻿—he could afford to make no mistake; and his soul, or whatever that thing may be called which is certainly not the body, was closing with Westray’s soul in a desperate struggle for mastery.
Westray was not seeing the picture for the first time, and after one glance he stood aloof. The interview was becoming even more painful than he had expected. He avoided looking Lord Blandamer in the face, yet presently, at a slight movement, turned and met his eye.
“Yes, it is my grandfather,” said the other.
There was nothing in the words, and yet it seemed to Westray as if some terrible confidence was being thrust upon him against his will; as if Lord Blandamer had abandoned any attempt to mislead, and was tacitly avowing all that might be charged against him. The architect began to feel that he was now regarded as a personal enemy, though he had never so considered himself. It was true that picture and papers had fallen into his hands, but he knew that a sense of duty was the only motive of any action that he might be taking.
“You promised, I think, to show me some papers,” Lord Blandamer said.
Most painfully Westray handed them over; his knowledge of their contents made it seem that he was offering a deliberate insult. He wished fervently that he never had made any proposal for this meeting; he ought to have given everything to the proper authorities, and have let the blow fall as it would. Such an interview could only end in bitterness: its present result was that here in Lord Blandamer’s own house, he, Westray, was presenting him with proofs of his father’s illegitimacy, with proofs that he had no right to this house﻿—no, nor to anything else.

===Chinese===

布兰达默勋爵天南地北都走过；他见过、做过离奇的事；他曾为性命作过只能一人生还的搏斗；可这里没有血肉的问题——他要面对的是原则，正是他指望借以缓刑的那几条原则；他要面对韦斯特雷的正直，那使劝说和贿赂同样无从下手。这幅画他从未见过；他凝神看了几分钟；可他的注意始终聚在身旁这个人的身上。这是他最后的机会——错不起一步；他的灵魂，或随你怎样称呼那件确乎不是肉身的东西——正同韦斯特雷的灵魂短兵相接，作一场争夺雄长的死斗。
韦斯特雷不是头一回见这幅画，一瞥之后就退在一边。这场会面变得比他预想的更痛苦。他避开布兰达默勋爵的脸；可片刻之后，对方微微一动，他转过脸，正撞上那双眼睛。
「是的，这是我的祖父，」那一位说。
话本身没有什么；可韦斯特雷只觉一份可怕的信托正违背他的意愿硬塞给他；仿佛布兰达默勋爵已放弃了任何瞒骗的打算，把一切可能加于己身的罪名默默招认了。建筑师开始觉得，对方如今把他当作私敌了，虽然他自己从无此想。画和文书确是落到他手里；可他知道，责任感是他一切行动的唯一动机。
「您答应过，我想，要给我看一些文书，」布兰达默勋爵说。
韦斯特雷把它们递过去，递得万般痛苦；他明知内容，递过去仿佛是存心的侮辱。他热切地希望自己从没提议过这场会面；他本该把一切交给正当的官署，听凭那一击落在何处。这样的会面只能以辛酸收场：眼下的局面竟是——在布兰达默勋爵自家的宅子里，他韦斯特雷正向主人奉上他父亲私生的证据，奉上证明他无权享有这座宅子——不，无权再享有任何东西——的证据。

===Original===

It was a bitter moment for Lord Blandamer to find such information in the possession of a younger man; but, if there was more colour in his face than usual, his self-command stood the test, and he thrust resentment aside. There was no time to say or do useless things, there was no time for feeling; all his attention must be concentrated on the man before him. He stood still, seeming to examine the papers closely, and, as a matter of fact, he did take note of the name, the place, and the date, that so many careful searchings had failed ever to find. But all the while he was resolutely considering the next move, and giving Westray time to think and feel. When he looked up, their eyes met again, and this time it was Westray that coloured.
“I suppose you have verified these certificates?” Lord Blandamer asked very quietly.
“Yes,” Westray said, and Lord Blandamer gave them back to him without a word, and walked slowly away down the gallery.
Westray crushed the papers into his pocket where most of the room was taken up by the pistol; he was glad to get them out of his sight; he could not bear to hold them. It was as if a beaten fighter had given up his sword. With these papers Lord Blandamer seemed to resign into his adversary’s hands everything of which he stood possessed, his lands, his life, the honour of his house. He made no defence, no denial, no resistance, least of all any appeal. Westray was left master of the situation, and must do whatever he thought fit. This fact was clearer to him now than it had ever been before, the secret was his alone; with him rested the responsibility of making it public. He stood dumb before the picture, from which the old lord looked at him with penetrating eyes. He had nothing to say; he could not go after Lord Blandamer; he wondered whether this was indeed to be the end of the interview, and turned sick at the thought of the next step that must be taken.
At the distance of a few yards Lord Blandamer paused, and looked round, and Westray understood that he was being invited, or commanded, to follow. They stopped opposite the portrait of a lady, but it was the frame to which Lord Blandamer called attention by laying his hand on it.
“This was my grandmother,” he said; “they were companion pictures. They are the same size, the moulding on the frame is the same, an interlacing fillet, and the coat of arms is in the same place.

===Chinese===

这样一桩秘密竟握在一个后生手里，对布兰达默勋爵是苦涩的一刻；可纵然他脸上比平常多了一层颜色，他的自制经住了考验，他把怨怼推开了。没有工夫说废话、做无用之事，没有工夫动感情；全部注意必须聚在眼前这个人身上。他站定不动，装作细看那些文书——事实上他也真的记下了那名字、那地点、那日期：多少人仔仔细细寻了多少年而不得的东西。可与此同时，他在坚决地盘算下一步，也留给韦斯特雷时间去想、去感受。他抬起头时，两人的目光又撞在一起；这一回脸红的是韦斯特雷。
「我想，这些证书您都核实过了？」布兰达默勋爵极为平静地问。
「是，」韦斯特雷说；布兰达默勋爵一言不发把它们递还给他，缓步沿画廊走开去。
韦斯特雷把那几页纸团进衣袋——衣袋大半被手枪占着；他巴不得它们从眼前消失，捏也捏不住。这像力竭的剑士交出了剑。随着这几页纸，布兰达默勋爵仿佛把他名下所有的一切——土地、性命、门第的荣誉——都让进了对手手里。他不辩白，不否认，不抵抗，更不哀求。韦斯特雷成了局面的主人，尽可以随意处置。这一点此刻他看得比任何时候都清楚：秘密是他一个人的；公布它，责任也落在他一个人身上。他哑立在那幅画前，老勋爵用洞察的眼睛望着他。他无话可说；他不能追上前去；他疑心会面是否就此了结，一想到必须迈出的下一步，只觉作呕。
隔着几码，布兰达默勋爵停住脚，回过头；韦斯特雷明白，这是在请他——或命令他——跟上去。两人在一幅淑女肖像前停住；布兰达默勋爵却把手搭在画框上，引他注意框子。
「这是我的祖母，」他说；「它们原是一对。尺寸相同，框上的线脚相同，都是交缠的饰条；纹章的位置也相同。

===Original===

You see?” he added, finding Westray still silent.
Westray was obliged to meet his look once more.
“I see,” he said, most reluctantly. He knew now, that the unusual moulding and the size of the picture that hung in Miss Joliffe’s house, must have revealed its identity long ago to the man who stood before him; that during all those visits in which plans for the church had been examined and discussed, Lord Blandamer must have known what lay hid under the flowers, must have known that the green wriggling caterpillar was but a bar of the nebuly coat. Confidences were being forced upon Westray that he could not forget, and could not reveal. He longed to cry out, “For God’s sake, do not tell me these things; do not give me this evidence against yourself!”
There was another short pause, and then Lord Blandamer turned. He seemed to expect Westray to turn with him, and they walked back over the soft carpet down the gallery in a silence that might be heard. The air was thick with doom; Westray felt as if he were stifling. He had lost mental control, his thoughts were swallowed up in a terrible chaos. Only one reflection stood out, the sense of undivided responsibility. It was not as if he were adding a link, as in duty bound, to a long chain of other evidence: the whole matter was at rest; to set it in motion again would be his sole act, his act alone. There was a refrain ringing in his ears, a verse that he had heard read a few Sundays before in Cullerne Church, “Am I God, to kill and make alive? Am I God, to kill and make alive?” Yet duty commanded him to go forward, and go forward he must, though the result was certain: he would be playing the part of executioner.
The man whose fate he must seal was keeping pace with him quietly, step by step. If he could only have a few moments to himself, he might clear his distracted thoughts. He paused before some other picture, feigning to examine it, but Lord Blandamer paused also, and looked at him. He knew Lord Blandamer’s eye was upon him, though he refused to return the look. It seemed a mere act of courtesy on Lord Blandamer’s part to stop. Mr. Westray might be specially interested in some of the pictures, and, if any information was required, it was the part of the host to see that it was forthcoming. Westray stopped again once or twice, but always with the same result.

===Chinese===

您看出来了？」他见韦斯特雷仍不作声，又加了一句。
韦斯特雷不得不又一次迎上他的目光。
「我看出来了，」他说，万般不愿。此刻他明白了：乔利夫小姐家那幅画异样的线脚和尺寸，必早在很久以前就向眼前这个人揭明了它的身份；在那些检阅图纸、讨论工程的历次来访里，布兰达默勋爵必早已知道花底下藏着什么，必早已知道那条蠕动的绿毛虫不过是尼布里纹章的一道横条。这些信托硬往韦斯特雷身上堆，堆得他忘不掉、又吐不出。他恨不得喊出来：「看在上帝份上，别告诉我这些；别把不利于你自己的证据塞给我！」
又短短停了片刻，布兰达默勋爵转过身。他似乎等着韦斯特雷同他一起转，两人便踏着软地毯沿画廊往回走，一片听得见的寂静。空气里稠稠地都是劫数；韦斯特雷觉得自己要窒息。他失去了对心神的控制，思绪被吞进一团可怕的混沌。只有一条念头凸出来：全责独任之感。这不像是循着本分、往一条长链上再添一环：整个事情如今是静着的；要再叫它动起来，将全然是他的行为，他一个人的行为。他耳边响着一句叠唱，几个星期日之前他在卡勒恩教堂听人诵过的：「我岂是神，能叫人死、叫人活么？我岂是神，能叫人死、叫人活么？」然而本分命令他向前；他也必须向前，纵然结局注定了：他将扮演刽子手。
那个命运要由他封死的人，正一步一步，安静地同他并着肩。倘若能容他独处片刻，他纷乱的思绪或可理清。他在另一幅画前停住，装作端详；布兰达默勋爵也停住，望着他。他知道那双眼睛盯着自己，却偏不回望。对方停下来，看着只像纯然的礼数：韦斯特雷先生也许对某几张画特别感兴趣，若有所垂询，做主人的理应奉告。韦斯特雷又停了一两回，回回都是这样。

===Original===

He did not know whether he was looking at portraits or landscapes, though he was vaguely aware that halfway down the gallery, there stood on the floor what seemed to be an unfinished picture, with its face turned to the wall.
Except when Westray stopped, Lord Blandamer looked neither to the right nor to the left; he walked with his hands folded lightly behind him, and with his eyes upon the ground, yet did not feign to have his thoughts disengaged. His companion shrank from any attempt to understand or fathom what those thoughts could be, but admired, against his will, the contained and resolute bearing. Westray felt as a child beside a giant, yet had no doubt as to his own duty, or that he was going to do it. But how hard it was! Why had he been so foolish as to meddle with the picture? Why had he read papers that did not belong to him? Why, above all, had he come down to Fording to have his suspicions confirmed? What business was it of his to ferret out these things? He felt all the unutterable aversion of an upright mind for playing the part of a detective; all the sovereign contempt even for such petty meanness as allows one person to examine the handwriting or postmark of letters addressed to another. Yet he knew this thing, and he alone; he could not do away with this horrible knowledge.
The end of the gallery was reached; they turned with one accord and paced slowly, silently back, and the time was slipping away fast. It was impossible for Westray to consider anything *now*, but he had taken his decision before he came to Fording; he must go through with it; there was no escape for *him* any more than for Lord Blandamer. He would keep his word. On Monday, the day he had mentioned, he would speak, and once begun, the matter would pass out of his hands. But how was he to tell this to the man who was walking beside him, and silently waiting for his sentence? He could not leave him in suspense; to do so would be cowardice and cruelty. He must make his intention clear, but how? in what form of words? There was no time to think; already they were repassing that canvas which stood with its face to the wall.
The suspense, the impenetrable silence, was telling upon Westray; he tried again to rearrange his thoughts, but they were centred only on Lord Blandamer. How calm he seemed, with his hands folded behind him, and never a finger twitching!

===Chinese===

他不知道自己看的是肖像还是风景，只约略觉出画廊半途的地板上立着一幅面朝墙壁的、似乎是未装框的画。
除韦斯特雷停下的时候，布兰达默勋爵目不斜视；双手轻轻拢在背后，眼睛望着地，却不装作心不在焉。他的同伴畏怯着，不敢去揣度那些心思，却身不由己地佩服那克制而决断的气度。韦斯特雷自觉像巨人脚边的孩子，对本分、对必行的决心却毫无疑惑。可是多难啊！他当时怎么那样傻，去动那幅画？怎么读了不属于他的文书？更怎么特地跑到福丁来，叫自己的疑心坐实？这些事同他什么相干，要他来刨根究底？一颗正直的心对扮演暗探的全部难言的憎恶，他全感到了；连那种窥看旁人信件的字迹和邮戳的小人行径，他也报以十足的鄙夷。可他知道了这件事，只有他一个人知道；这可怕的知识，他销毁不掉。
画廊到了头；两人不约而同转身，缓缓地、默默地踱回；时间飞快地溜走。韦斯特雷此刻已不可能再考虑什么；可来福丁之前他已拿定主意：他必须做到底；他和布兰达默勋爵一样，都没有退路了。他将守约。到了他提过的那个星期一，他就开口；一开了头，事情就出他的手了。可这话怎样对身旁这个默默候判的人说呢？总不能叫他悬着；那样做是怯懦，是残忍。他必须把自己的打算说明白；可怎么说明白？用什么字眼？没有工夫想了；那幅面朝墙壁的画，他们又走过去了。
这悬而未决，这穿不透的沉默，压得韦斯特雷透不过气；他再想理一遍思绪，思绪却只聚在布兰达默勋爵一个人身上。他显得多平静啊，双手拢在背后，一根指头也不动！

===Original===

What did *he* mean to do﻿—to fly, or kill himself, or stand his ground and take his trial on a last chance? It would be a celebrated trial. Hateful and inevitable details occurred to Westray’s imagination: the crowded, curious court as he saw it in his dream, with Lord Blandamer in the dock, and this last thought sickened him. His own place would be in the witness-box. Incidents that he wished to forget would be recalled, discussed, dwelt on; he would have to search his memory for them, narrate them, swear to them. But this was not all. He would have to give an account of this very afternoon’s work. It could not be hushed up. Every servant in the house would know how he had come to Fording with a picture. He heard himself cross-examined as to “this very remarkable interview.” What account was he to give of it? What a betrayal of confidence it would be to give *any* account. Yet he must, and his evidence would be given under the eyes of Lord Blandamer in the dock. Lord Blandamer would be in the dock watching him. It was unbearable, impossible; rather than this he would fly himself, he would use the pistol that bulged his pocket against his own life.
Lord Blandamer had noted Westray’s nervous movements, his glances to right and left, as though seeking some way of escape; he saw the clenched hands, and the look of distress as they paced to and fro. He knew that each pause before a picture was an attempt to shake him off, but he would not be shaken off; Westray was feeling the grip, and must not have a moment’s breathing space. He could tell exactly how the minutes were passing, he knew what to listen for, and could catch the distant sound of the stable clock striking the quarters. They were back at the end of the gallery. There was no time to pace it again; Westray must go now if he was to catch his train.
They stopped opposite the old lord’s portrait; the silence wrapped Westray round, as the white fog had wrapped him round that night on his way to Cullerne Road. He wanted to speak, but his brain was confused, his throat was dry; he dreaded the sound of his own voice.
Lord Blandamer took out his watch.
“I have no wish to hurry you, Mr. Westray,” he said, “but your train leaves Lytchett in little over an hour. It will take you nearly that time to drive to the station. May I help you to repack this picture?”

===Chinese===

*他*打算怎样——逃，自尽，还是站稳了受审、搏最后一线生机？那将是一场轰动的审判。可憎而又免不了的细节，一一浮上韦斯特雷的想象：那座他梦里见过的、拥挤而好奇的法庭，被告席里的布兰达默勋爵——想到这里他直欲作呕。他自己将站在证人席上。他巴不得忘掉的种种情节，将被一一追述、讨论、把玩；他须从记忆里搜出它们，叙述它们，宣誓作证。这还不算。他还得交代这天下午的一切。这是遮掩不住的。宅子里每个仆人都会知道他怎样带着一幅画来福丁。他听见自己被盘问「这次极不寻常的会面」的始末。他拿什么交代？无论怎样交代，都是怎样的出卖信托！可他必须交代，而且要在被告席里布兰达默勋爵的注视之下。布兰达默勋爵将坐在被告席里看着他。这叫人不能忍受，不能想像；与其如此，他宁可自己逃走，宁可拿衣袋里那支鼓鼓的手枪，对准自己的性命。
布兰达默勋爵早已留意韦斯特雷紧张的举动、他左顾右盼像寻出路的目光；看见那紧攝的双手、来回踱步时脸上的苦相。他知道在每一幅画前的停顿都是想甩脱他；可他不肯被甩脱；韦斯特雷正觉出那钳子，就一刻也不容他喘息。他掐得准分寸的流逝，知道该听什么，听得见远处马厩的钟报刻的声息。他们又踱回了画廊尽头。再没有时间踱一个来回；韦斯特雷要赶车，此刻就得走。
两人在老勋爵的肖像对面停住；寂静裹住韦斯特雷，像那夜去卡勒恩路时白雾裹住他一样。他想说话，可脑中混乱，喉咙发干；他怕听见自己的嗓音。
布兰达默勋爵掏出表。
「我不愿催您，韦斯特雷先生，」他说，「可您的火车一个钟头多一点就离利切特了。车赶到站上差不多也要这些时间。要我帮您把画重新包上吗？」

===Original===

His voice was clear, level, and courteous, as on the day when Westray had first met him at Bellevue Lodge. The silence was broken, and Westray found himself speaking quickly in answer:
“You invited me to stay here for the night. I have changed my mind, and will accept your offer, if I may.” He hesitated for a moment, and then went on: “I shall be thankful if you will keep the picture and these documents. I see now that I have no business with them.”
He took the crumpled papers from his pocket, and held them out without looking up.
Then silence fell on them again, and Westray’s heart stood still; till after a second that seemed an eternity Lord Blandamer took the papers with a short “I thank you,” and walked a little way further, to the end of the gallery. The architect leant against the side of a window opposite which he found himself, and, looking out without seeing anything, presently heard Lord Blandamer tell a servant that Mr. Westray would stop the night, and that wine was to be brought them in the gallery. In a few minutes the man came back with a decanter on a salver, and Lord Blandamer filled glasses for Westray, and himself. He felt probably that both needed something of the kind, but to the other more was implied. Westray remembered that an hour ago he had refused to eat or drink under this roof. An hour ago﻿—how his mood had changed in that short time! How he had flung duty and principle to the winds! Surely this glass of red wine was a very sacrament of the devil, which made him a partner of iniquity.
As he raised the glass to his lips a slanting sunbeam shot through the window, and made the wine glow red as blood. The drinkers paused glass in hand, and glancing up saw the red sun setting behind the trees in the park. Then the old lord’s picture caught the evening light, the green bars of the nebuly coat danced before Westray’s eyes, till they seemed to live, to be again three wriggling caterpillars, and the penetrating grey eyes looked out from the canvas as if they were watching the enactment of this final scene. Lord Blandamer pledged him in a bumper, and Westray answered without hesitation, for he had given his allegiance, and would have drunk poison in token that there was to be no turning back now.
An engagement kept Lady Blandamer from home that evening. Lord Blandamer had intended to accompany her, but afterwards told her that Mr.

===Chinese===

他的嗓音清朗、平稳、客气，同那天在美景小筑初见韦斯特雷时一样。寂静破了，韦斯特雷听见自己急急作答：
「您曾邀我在这儿过夜。我改了主意，愿意领受您的款待，如果可以。」他顿了顿，又接着说：「您若肯收下这幅画和这些文书，我感激不尽。我如今明白了，它们与我无干。」
他从衣袋里掏出那几页揉皱的纸，递过去，头也不抬。
随后两人又陷入沉默；韦斯特雷的心停了摆；过了仿佛一世的一秒，布兰达默勋爵接过纸，短短说声「谢谢你」，再往画廊尽头走了几步。建筑师凭在身旁一扇窗的窗边，望着窗外什么也没看见，只听布兰达默勋爵吩咐仆人：韦斯特雷先生今晚住下；把酒送到画廊来。几分钟后仆人端着托盘和酒瓶回来，布兰达默勋爵为韦斯特雷和自己各斟了一杯。他大约觉得两人都需要这一口；可对那一位，这里面含的更多。韦斯特雷记起，一个钟头前他还拒绝在这屋顶下饮食。一个钟头前——短短一个钟头，他的心境变了几变！本分和原则，被他抛到了怎样九霄云外！这杯红酒岂不分明是一场魔鬼的圣餐，叫他入了共罪的伙？
他把杯举到唇边时，一道斜阳穿过窗子，把酒照得血红。两个持杯的人都停住了；抬头一望，见通红的日轮正落在园中树后。老勋爵的画像也接住了暮光，尼布里纹章的绿条在韦斯特雷眼前舞动，直到仿佛活了起来，又成了三条蠕动的毛虫；那双洞察的灰眼睛从画布上望出来，仿佛正注视着这最后一幕的上演。布兰达默勋爵满杯向他一举；韦斯特雷毫不迟疑地应了——他已投了效，此刻便是鸩酒，他也要喝下去，为的是表明再无回头。
那天晚上一桩应酬使布兰达默夫人不在家。布兰达默勋爵本要陪她同去，后来告诉她韦斯特雷先生有要事将到，她便独自去了。晚饭只有布兰达默勋爵同韦斯特雷两个人；主人举止里一点微妙的变

===Original===

Westray was coming on important business, and so she went alone. Only Lord Blandamer and Westray sat down to dinner, and some subtle change of manner made the architect conscious that for the first time since their acquaintance, his host was treating him as a real equal. Lord Blandamer maintained a flow of easy and interesting conversation, yet never approached the subject of architecture even near enough to seem to be avoiding it. After dinner he took Westray to the library, where he showed him some old books, and used all his art to entertain him and set him at his ease. Westray was soothed for a moment by the other’s manner, and did his best to respond to the courtesy shown him; but everything had lost its savour, and he knew that black Care was only waiting for him to be alone, to make herself once more mistress of his being.
A wind which had risen after sunset began to blow near bedtime with unusual violence. The sudden gusts struck the library windows till they rattled again, and puffs of smoke came out from the fireplace into the room.
“I shall sit up for Lady Blandamer,” said the host, “but I dare say you will not be sorry to turn in;” and Westray, looking at his watch, saw that it wanted but ten minutes of midnight.
In the hall, and on the staircase, as they went up, the wind blowing with cold rushes made itself felt still more strongly.
“It is a wild night,” Lord Blandamer said, as he stopped for a moment before a barometer, “but I suspect that there is yet worse to come; the glass has fallen in an extraordinary way. I hope you have left all snug with the tower at Cullerne; this wind will not spare any weak places.”
“I don’t think it should do any mischief at Saint Sepulchre’s,” Westray answered, half unconsciously. It seemed as though he could not concentrate his thought even upon his work.
His bedroom was large, and chilly in spite of a bright fire. He locked the door, and drawing an easy-chair before the hearth, sat a long while in thought. It was the first time in his life that he had with deliberation acted against his convictions, and there followed the reaction and remorse inseparable from such conditions.
Is there any depression so deep as this? is there any night so dark as this first eclipse of the soul, this *first* conscious stilling of the instinct for right?

===Chinese===

化，叫建筑师觉出：相识以来头一回，主人是拿他当真正的对等者相待。布兰达默勋爵滔滔不绝地谈些轻松有趣的话，却始终不走近建筑这个题目，连若即若离地擦边都仿佛无意。饭后他领韦斯特雷进书房，给他看几种旧书，使出全副本事娱乐他、宽他的心。韦斯特雷一时被这份态度抚慰了，竭力酬答这份礼遇；可一切都失了味，他知道，黑色的忧愁只等他一独处，就要重新做他心灵的主宰。
日落之后起的风，近就寝时刮得异样地猛。一阵阵狂风打得书房窗子哗哗作响，一股股烟从壁炉倒灌进屋来。
「我要坐着等布兰达默夫人，」主人说，「不过我想您也乐于早些安置；」韦斯特雷看看表，离半夜只差十分钟了。
上楼时，穿堂和楼梯上，风一阵阵带着寒气扑面，觉来愈加分明。
「是个狂风之夜，」布兰达默勋爵在一具晴雨计前停了停，说，「只怕更坏的还在后头；表针落得蹊跷。卡勒恩那边塔上的事，您都安置妥帖了吧？这样的风，不会饶过任何薄弱的地方。」
「我想圣墓教堂不至于出事，」韦斯特雷半无意识地答。他仿佛连自己的本行都不能集中心思去想了。
他的卧室宽大，炉火虽旺，仍有寒意。他锁上门，拉一把软椅到炉前，久久坐着出神。这是他平生第一次蓄意行事、违背自己的信念；随之而来的，是这种情形免不了的反弹与悔恨。
还有比这更深的消沉么？还有比这更黑的夜么——灵魂第一次蚀灭，正当感*头一回*被有意地按熄？

===Original===

He had conspired to obscure truth, he had made himself partaker in another man’s wrongdoing, and, as the result, he had lost his moral foothold, his self-respect, his self-reliance. It was true that, even if he could, he would not have changed his decision now, yet the weight of a guilty secret, that he must keep all his life long, pressed heavily upon him. Something must be done to lighten this weight; he must take some action that would ease the galling of his thoughts. He was in that broken mood for which the Middle Ages offered the cloister as a remedy; he felt the urgent need of sacrifice and abnegation to purge him. And then he knew the sacrifice that he must make: he must give up his work at Cullerne. He was thankful to find that there was still enough of conscience left to him to tell him this. He could not any longer be occupied on work for which the money was being found by this man. He would give up his post at Cullerne, even if it meant giving up his connection with his employers, even if it meant the giving up of his livelihood. He felt as if England itself were not large enough to hold him and Lord Blandamer. He must never more see the associate of his guilt; he dreaded meeting his eyes again, lest the other’s will should constrain his will to further wrong. He would write to resign his work the very next day; that would be an active sacrifice, a definite mark from which he might begin a painful retracing of the way, a turning-point from which he might hope in time to recover some measure of self-respect and peace of mind. He would resign his work at Cullerne the very next day; and then a wilder gust of wind buffeted the windows of his room, and he thought of the scaffolding on Saint Sepulchre’s tower. What a terrible night it was! Would the thin bows of the tower arches live through such a night, with the weight of the great tower rocking over them? No, he could not resign tomorrow. It would be deserting his post. He must stand by till the tower was safe, *that* was his first duty. After that he would give up his post at once.
Later on he went to bed, and in those dark watches of the night, that are not kept by reason, there swept over him thoughts wilder than the wind outside. He had made himself sponsor for Lord Blandamer, he had assumed the burden of the other’s crime.

===Chinese===

他参与了遮蔽真相的同谋；他使自己成了他人罪行的伙同者；结果，他失去了道德的立脚点，失去了自尊，失去了自信。不错，纵然此刻能够，他也不愿更改自己的决定；可那件必须保守终身的罪秘，沉沉压在他身上。总得做点什么减轻这重量；总得有个行动，缓一缓思绪的磨痛。他正处在中世纪拿修道院作解药的那种破碎心境里；他切切感到需要牺牲、需要自苦来净化自己。随即他明白了该作什么牺牲：他必须放弃卡勒恩的工作。他庆幸自己良心尚存，还能告诉他这一点。他不能再做由这个人出钱的工作。他要辞去卡勒恩的职务，哪怕这意味着同雇主断绝关系，哪怕这意味着丢掉饭碗。他觉得英格兰都不够大，装不下他同布兰达默勋爵两个人。他再不能见到这个共罪的伙伴；他怕再见那双眼睛，怕对方的意志又辖制他的意志，去作更大的恶。第二天他就写信辞工；那将是一个主动的祭献，一块清晰的界石，从那里开始一段痛苦的回溯，一个转折点，从那里，他或可指望渐渐赎回几分自尊和心境的安宁。第二天一早就辞去卡勒恩的工作——这时一阵更狂的风撞在他房间的窗上，他想起了圣墓教堂塔上的脚手架。多可怕的夜啊！大塔的重荷在塔基拱那几道细弱的弓上摇撼，它们熬得过这样的夜么？不，明天辞不得工。那是临阵脱逃。他必须守到塔身安全；*那*才是他的第一本分。那以后再立刻去职。
后来他上了床；在那不由理性守夜的黑暗更次里，比外头狂风更狂的念头一阵阵扫过他。他做了布兰达默勋爵的保人；他把对方的罪担在了自己肩上。

===Original===

It was he that was branded with the mark of Cain, and he must hide it in silence from the eyes of all men. He must fly from Cullerne, and walk alone with his burden for the rest of his life, a scapegoat in the isolation of the wilderness.
In sleep the terror that walketh in darkness brooded heavily on him. He was in the church of Saint Sepulchre, and blood dripped on him from the organ-loft. Then as he looked up to find out whence it came he saw the four tower arches falling to grind him to powder, and leapt up in his bed, and struck a light to make sure that there were no red patches on him. With daylight he grew calmer. The wild visions vanished, but the cold facts remained: he was sunk in his own esteem, he had forced himself into an evil secret which was no concern of his, and now he must keep it forever.
Westray found Lady Blandamer in the breakfast-room. Lord Blandamer had met her in the hall on her return the night before, and though he was pale, she knew before he had spoken half a dozen words, that the cloud of anxiety which had hung heavily on him for the last few days was past. He told her that Mr. Westray had come over on business, and, in view of the storm that was raging, had been persuaded to remain for the night. The architect had brought with him a picture which he had accidentally come across, a portrait of the old Lord Blandamer which had been missing for many years from Fording. It was very satisfactory that it had been recovered; they were under a great obligation to Mr. Westray for the trouble which he had taken in the matter.
In the events of the preceding days Westray had almost forgotten Lady Blandamer’s existence, and since the discovery of the picture, if her image presented itself to his mind, it had been as that of a deeply wronged and suffering woman. But this morning she appeared with a look of radiant content that amazed him, and made him shudder as he thought how near he had been only a day before to plunging her into the abyss. The more careful nurture of the year that had passed since her marriage, had added softness to her face and figure, without detracting from the refinement of expression that had always marked her. He knew that she was in her own place, and wondered now that the distinction of her manner had not led him sooner to the truth of her birth.

===Chinese===

带该隐记号的是他；他必须把它默然遮住，不给世人的眼睛看见。他必须逃离卡勒恩，余生独自负着这担子行走——荒野里离群索居的一头替罪羊。
睡梦里，黑暗中游行的恐怖重重伏在他身上。他在圣墓教堂里；血从管风琴楼上滴在他身上。他抬头寻那血的来处，只见四道塔基拱正塌下来，要把他碾成齑粉；他从床上惊起，擦亮火柴，查看身上有没有红斑。天亮后他平静了些。狂乱的幻象散了，冰冷的事实还在：他在自己的估量里一落千丈；他强行闯进了一桩与他无干的罪秘，如今必须守它一辈子。
韦斯特雷在早餐室见到布兰达默夫人。前一晚她回来时，布兰达默勋爵在门厅迎着她；他虽脸色苍白，可不出半打句话她就知道：几天来沉沉压着他的那团焦虑，过去了。他告诉她，韦斯特雷先生为要事来访，赶上风狂雨骤，被留下过夜。建筑师带来一幅他偶然寻得的画——失踪多年的老布兰达默勋爵的肖像，福丁失之物。失而复得，实在可喜；韦斯特雷先生为此费的心，他们领情非浅。
连日种种，韦斯特雷几乎忘了布兰达默夫人的存在；自发现那幅画以来，她的形象若来到他心里，也总是一个被深深错待、受着苦的女人。可今天早晨，她带着一脸光彩的满足出现，叫他愕然，又叫他不寒而栗——一天之前，他离把她抛下深渊，曾那样近。婚后这一年更精细的调养，给她的脸和身段添了柔润，却不减她向来那份眉目间的清雅。他明白了：她如今在自己该在的位置；此刻更奇怪，她举止里那份气韵，竟没有更早点破她的出身，引他识破真相。

===Original===

She looked pleased to meet him, and shook hands with a frank smile that acknowledged their former relations, without any trace of embarrassment. It seemed incredible that she should ever have brought him up his meals and letters.
She made a polite reference to his having restored to them an interesting family picture, and finding him unexpectedly embarrassed, changed the subject by asking him what he thought of her own portrait.
“I think you must have seen it yesterday,” she went on, as he appeared not to understand. “It has only just come home, and is standing on the floor in the long gallery.”
Lord Blandamer glanced at the architect, and answered for him that Mr. Westray had not seen it. Then he explained with a composure that shed a calm through the room:
“It was turned to the wall. It is a pity to show it unhung, and without a frame. We must get it framed at once, and decide on a position for it. I think we shall have to shift several paintings in the gallery.”
He talked of Snyders and Wouverman, and Westray made some show of attention, but could only think of the unframed picture standing on the ground, which had helped to measure the passing of time in the terrible interview of yesterday. He guessed now that Lord Blandamer had himself turned the picture with its face to the wall, and in doing so had deliberately abandoned a weapon that might have served him well in the struggle. Lord Blandamer must have deliberately foregone the aid of recollections such as Anastasia’s portrait would have called up in his antagonist’s mind. “*Non tali auxilio nec defensoribus istis.*”
Westray’s haggard air had not escaped his host’s notice. The architect looked as if he had spent the night in a haunted room, and Lord Blandamer was not surprised, knowing that the other’s scruples had died hard, and were not likely to lie quiet in their graves. He thought it better that the short time which remained before Westray’s departure should be spent out of the house, and proposed a stroll in the grounds. The gardener reported, he said, that last night’s gale had done considerable damage to the trees. The top of the cedar on the south lawn had been broken short off. Lady Blandamer begged that she might accompany them, and as they walked down the terrace steps into the garden a nurse brought to her the baby heir.
“The gale must have been a cyclone,” Lord Blandamer said.

===Chinese===

她见到他显得高兴，带着坦然的微笑同他握手，承认旧日的那层关系，没有一丝窘迫。说她曾给他端过饭、送过信，如今想来恍如不可能。
她客气地提到他为他们寻回了一幅有趣的家族画像，见他意外地发窘，便转过话头，问他觉得她自己的画像如何。
「我想您昨天一定见过了，」见他似乎不解，她又往下说。「它刚到家，正搁在长廊的地板上。」
布兰达默勋爵瞟了建筑师一眼，替他答道：韦斯特雷先生没有见到。随后他以一种把满屋照得澄静的从容，解释道：
「它面朝墙立着。没挂起来、没有框就示人，可惜了。得马上配框，再定个位置。画廊里有几幅，怕都要挪一挪。」
他谈起斯尼德斯和韦弗曼；韦斯特雷装出在听的样子，心里只有那幅无框立在地板上的画——昨天那场可怕的会面里，它曾帮着计量时间的流逝。此刻他猜到了：是布兰达默勋爵亲手把那幅画翻转面壁的；这一翻，就是存心放下了本可在搏斗里大派用场的一件兵器。安娜斯塔西娅的肖像会在对手心里唤起怎样的回忆，布兰达默勋爵必是存心弃绝了这奥援。*Non tali auxilio nec defensoribus istis*（不要这样的援助，也不要这样的援手）。
韦斯特雷的憔悴没有逃过主人的眼睛。建筑师那副样子，像在闹鬼的房里坐了一夜；布兰达默勋爵并不奇怪——他知道，此人的顾忌死得艰难，进了坟墓也未必安分。他想，韦斯特雷行前这点短短的时光，还是消在宅子外面为好，便提议到园子里走走。他说，园丁来报，昨夜的烈风伤了树。南草坪那株雪松，顶子整个折断了。布兰达默夫人恳求同行；三人沿台地石阶下进花园时，奶妈把襁褓里的小继承人抱了给她。
「这风定是一场旋风，」布兰达默勋爵说。

===Original===

“It has passed away as suddenly as it arose.”
The morning was indeed still and sunshiny, and seemed more beautiful by contrast with the turmoil of the previous night. The air was clear and cold after the rain, but paths and lawns were strewn with broken sticks and boughs, and carpeted with prematurely fallen leaves.
Lord Blandamer described the improvements that he was making or projecting, and pointed out the old fishponds which were to be restocked, the bowling-green and the ladies’ garden arranged on an old-world plan by his grandmother, and maintained unchanged since her death. He had received an immense service from Westray, and he would not accept it ungraciously or make little of it. In taking the architect round the place, in showing this place that his ancestors had possessed for so many generations, in talking of his plans for a future that had only so recently become assured, he was in a manner conveying his thanks, and Westray knew it.
Lady Blandamer was concerned for Westray. She saw that he was downcast, and ill at ease, and in her happiness that the cloud had passed from her husband, she wanted everyone to be happy with her. So, as they were returning to the house, she began, in the kindness of her heart, to talk of Cullerne Minster. She had a great longing, she said, to see the old church again. She should so much enjoy it if Mr. Westray would some day show her over it. Would he take much longer in the restorations?
They were in an alley too narrow for three to walk abreast. Lord Blandamer had fallen behind, but was within earshot.
Westray answered quickly, without knowing what he was going to say. He was not sure about the restorations﻿—that was, they certainly were not finished; in fact, they would take some time longer, but he would not be there, he believed, to superintend them. That was to say, he was giving up his present appointment.
He broke off, and Lady Blandamer knew that she had again selected an unfortunate subject. She dropped it, and hoped he would let them know when he was next at leisure, and come for a longer visit.
“I am afraid it will not be in my power to do so,” Westray said; and then, feeling that he had given a curt and ungracious answer to a kindly-meant invitation, turned to her and explained with unmistakable sincerity that he was giving up his connection with Farquhar and Farquhar.

===Chinese===

「来得突兀，去得也突兀。」
早晨实在安谧晴朗，比照昨夜的翻腾，愈显得美。雨后的空气清冽；小径和草坪上却铺满了断枝残柈，落了一地早凋的叶。
布兰达默勋爵讲着他正在办、正筹划的种种改良，指给他看待重新放养的旧鱼塘，保龄球场，和他祖母按古法经营、自她去世后一仍其旧的女人花园。韦斯特雷于他有再造之恩；他不肯领得没有气度，也不肯淡然处之。领着建筑师走遍这园宅，给他看祖上世代相守的这份基业，谈着这刚刚才得保住的将来的种种打算——这在他也是一种道谢；韦斯特雷心里明白。
布兰达默夫人关切着韦斯特雷。她看他意气消沉、坐立不安；丈夫头上的阴云散了，她满心欢喜，便盼人人都同她一起欢喜。于是回宅的路上，她出于心地的厚道，说起卡勒恩大教堂来。她说，她多么想再看看那座老教堂；哪天韦斯特雷先生肯领她细细走一遍，她不知多乐意。修复工程还要很久么？
小径窄得容不下三人并行。布兰达默勋爵落在后面，却还听得见。
韦斯特雷不假思索地急急作答——不知自己会说出什么。修复的事，他说不准——就是说，当然还没有完；实在还要好些时候；只是他相信自己不会在那儿督工了。这就是说，他正要辞去现在的职位。
他刹住了；布兰达默夫人知道自己又拣了个不巧的题目。她丢开它，只盼他得闲时知会一声，来多住几天。
「只怕我做不到了，」韦斯特雷说；随后觉得对一片好意回得太生硬，又转向她，带着不容误会的诚恳解释：他正要同法夸尔-法夸尔事务所断绝关系。

===Original===

This subject also was not to be pursued, so she only said that she was sorry, and her eyes confirmed her words.
Lord Blandamer was pained at what he had heard. He knew Farquhar and Farquhar, and knew something of Westray’s position and prospects﻿—that he had a reasonable income, and a promising future with the firm. This resolve must be quite sudden, a result of yesterday’s interview. Westray was being driven out into the wilderness like a scapegoat with another man’s guilt on his head. The architect was young and inexperienced. Lord Blandamer wished he could talk with him quietly. He understood that Westray might find it impossible to go on with the restoration at Cullerne, where all was being done at Lord Blandamer’s expense. But why sever his connection with a leading firm? Why not plead ill-health, nervous breakdown, those doctor’s orders which have opened a way of escape from impasses of the mind as well as of the body? An archaeologic tour in Spain, a yachting cruise in the Mediterranean, a winter in Egypt﻿—all these things would be to Westray’s taste; the blameless herb nepenthe might anywhere be found growing by the wayside. He must amuse himself, and forget. He wished he could *assure* Westray that he would forget, or grow used to remembering; that time heals wounds of conscience as surely as it heals heart-wounds and flesh-wounds; that remorse is the least permanent of sentiments. But then Westray might not yet wish to forget. He had run full counter to his principles. It might be that he was resolved to take the consequences, and wear them like a hair-shirt, as the only means of recovering his self-esteem. No; whatever penance, voluntary or involuntary, Westray might undergo, Lord Blandamer could only look on in silence. His object had been gained. If Westray felt it necessary to pay the price, he must be let pay it. Lord Blandamer could neither inquire nor remonstrate. He could offer no compensation, because no compensation would be accepted.
The little party were nearing the house when a servant met them.
“There is a man come over from Cullerne, my lord,” he said. “He is anxious to see Mr. Westray at once on important business.”
“Show him into my sitting-room, and say that Mr. Westray will be with him immediately.”
Westray met Lord Blandamer in the hall a few minutes later.

===Chinese===

这个题目也问不下去了；她只说了声可惜，眼睛证实了她的话。
布兰达默勋爵听了心里痛。他认识法夸尔-法夸尔，也约略知道韦斯特雷的地位和前程——有像样的进项，在事务所里有远大的将来。这决心必是突如其来的，是昨天那场会面的结果。韦斯特雷正像一头替罪羊，头上顶着别人的罪，被赶进旷野。建筑师年轻，历练浅。布兰达默勋爵但愿能同他静静谈一谈。他明白，韦斯特雷或许没法再办卡勒恩的修复——那里一切都是他布兰达默出资；可为什么同一流的事务所断绝？为什么不推托健康、神经衰弱——医生的嘱咐向来是身心两途的绝境都借以脱身的出口？去西班牙作考古之游，去地中海泛舟，去埃及过冬——这些都合韦斯特雷的口味；无罪的那味忘忧草，路旁随处可以寻得。他必须消遣，必须忘却。他但愿能*向*韦斯特雷担保：他会忘却，或者惯于记起；时间医良心的创，同医心伤、医外伤一样稳；悔恨是一切情感里最不长久的。可韦斯特雷也许还不想忘。他一头撞碎在自己的原则上。他也许打定了主意要领受后果，像穿苦修毛布衣那样穿着它们，当作赎回自尊的唯一法门。不；无论韦斯特雷自愿或非自愿地行什么苦赎，布兰达默勋爵都只能默然旁观。他的目的已经达到。韦斯特雷若觉得必须付这个价，就由他付罢。布兰达默勋爵既不能问，也不能劝。他提不出任何补偿，因为任何补偿都不会被接受。
一行人快到宅前时，一个仆人迎上来。
「有一位从卡勒恩来的，大人，」他说。「有要事，急着立刻见韦斯特雷先生。」
「领他到我的起居室去，就说韦斯特雷先生马上到。」
几分钟后，韦斯特雷在门厅见到布兰达默勋爵。

===Original===

“I am sorry to say there is bad news from Cullerne,” the architect said hurriedly. “Last night’s gale has strained and shaken the tower severely. A very serious movement is taking place. I must get back at once.”
“Do, by all means. A carriage is at the door. You can catch the train at Lytchett, and be in Cullerne by midday.”
The episode was a relief to Lord Blandamer. The architect’s attention was evidently absorbed in the tower. It might be that he had already found the blameless herb growing by the wayside.
The nebuly coat shone on the panel of the carriage-door. Lady Blandamer had noticed that her husband had been paying Westray special attention. He was invariably courteous, but he had treated this guest as he treated few others. Yet now, at the last moment, he had fallen silent; he was standing, she fancied, aloof. He held his hands behind him, and the attitude seemed to her to have some significance. But on Lord Blandamer’s part it was a mark of consideration. There had been no shaking of hands up to the present; he was anxious not to force Westray to take his hand by offering it before his wife and the servants.
Lady Blandamer felt that there was something going on which she did not understand, but she took leave of Westray with special kindness. She did not directly mention the picture, but said how much they were obliged to him, and glanced for confirmation at Lord Blandamer. He looked at Westray, and said with deliberation:
“I trust Mr. Westray knows how fully I appreciate his generosity and courtesy.”
There was a moment’s pause, and then Westray offered his hand. Lord Blandamer shook it cordially, and their eyes met for the last time.

===Chinese===

「抱歉，卡勒恩来了坏消息，」建筑师急急地说。「昨夜的烈风把塔摇撼得不轻。正在发生非常严重的走动。我必须立刻赶回去。」
「务必请便。车就在门口。您能在利切特赶上车，中午前到卡勒恩。」
这场插曲倒叫布兰达默勋爵松了口气。建筑师的注意显然全在塔上。忘忧草，也许他已经寻着了，就在路旁。
车门板上闪耀着尼布里纹章。布兰达默夫人早注意到丈夫对韦斯特雷格外殷勤。他向来对谁都有礼，但这位客人受的礼遇，连极少几人得过。可此刻到了最后关头，他沉默了；她觉得，他站得远远的。他双手拢在背后；那姿态在她眼里似乎有某种意味。可在布兰达默勋爵，那是一种体贴：至今两人没有握过手；他急于不要在妻子和仆人面前先伸出手，逼得韦斯特雷不能不握。
布兰达默夫人觉出这里头有一桩她不明白的事，但她同韦斯特雷道别时格外亲厚。她没有直接提那幅画，只说他们多么领他的情，又向布兰达默勋爵递去求证的一瞥。他望着韦斯特雷，字斟句酌地说：
「我相信，韦斯特雷先生知道，我是怎样完全领受他的慷慨与厚道。」
停了片刻，韦斯特雷伸出手来。布兰达默勋爵热诚地握住；两人的目光最后相接了一回。

## XXIII / 二十三
===Original===

On the afternoon of the same day Lord Blandamer was himself in Cullerne. He went to the office of Mr. Martelet, solicitor by prescriptive right to the family at Fording, and spent an hour closeted with the principal.
The house which the solicitor used for offices, was a derelict residence at the bottom of the town. It still had in front of it an extinguisher for links, and a lamp-bracket over the door of wasted iron scroll-work. It was a dingy place, but Mr. Martelet had a famous county connection, and rumour said that more important family business was done here even than in Carisbury itself. Lord Blandamer sat behind the dusty windows.
“I think I quite understand the nature of the codicil,” the solicitor said. “I will have a draft forwarded to your lordship tomorrow.”
“No, no; it is short enough. Let us finish with it now,” said his client. “There is no time like the present. It can be witnessed here. Your head clerk is discreet, is he not?”
“Mr. Simpkin has been with me thirty years,” the solicitor said deprecatingly, “and I have had no reason to doubt his discretion hitherto.”
The sun was low when Lord Blandamer left Mr. Martelet’s office. He walked down the winding street that led to the marketplace, with his long shadow going before him on the pavement. Above the houses in the near distance stood up the great tower of Saint Sepulchre’s, pink-red in the sunset rays. What a dying place was Cullerne! How empty were the streets! The streets were certainly strangely empty. He had never seen them so deserted. There was a silence of the grave over all. He took out his watch. The little place is gone to tea, he thought, and walked on with a light heart, and more at his ease than he had ever felt before in his life.
He came round a bend in the street, and suddenly saw a great crowd before him, between him and the marketplace over which the minster church watched, and knew that something must be happening, that had drawn the people from the other parts of the town. As he came nearer it seemed as if the whole population was there collected. Conspicuous was pompous Canon Parkyn, and by him stood Mrs. Parkyn, and tall and sloping-shouldered Mr. Noot. The sleek dissenting minister was there, and the jovial, round-faced Catholic priest.

===Chinese===

同一天下午，布兰达默勋爵本人到了卡勒恩。他走进马特莱先生的律师事务所——这位律师，凭沿袭的权利，是福丁家的顾问——同主任在里间密谈了一个钟头。
律师用作事务所的宅子，是镇子尽头一座废弃的旧邸。门前还立着一座火把熄灭罩，门上悬着一副累赘铁卷花枝的灯架。地方灰扑扑的，可马特莱先生有着闻名的郡中人脉，传闻连卡里斯伯里本地，也没有比这里经手的重要家族事务更多。布兰达默勋爵坐在积尘的窗后。
“遗嘱附录的性质，我想我完全明白了，”律师说，“明天我派人把稿子呈送爵爷。”
“不，不；它够短的。现在就办完吧，”委托人说，“再没有比当下更好的时候了。就在这儿签字见证也行。你的主任文书嘴严吧？”
“辛普金先生跟我三十年了，”律师谦逊地说，“至今我没有理由怀疑他的口风。”
布兰达默勋爵走出马特莱的事务所时，日头已经低了。他顺着通往集市的蜿蜒街道走下去，长长的影子先行投在人行道上。不远处，圣墓教堂的大塔在屋宇之上耸立，在落日里泛着绯红。卡勒恩是个多么死气的地方！街道多么空！街道确实空得出奇。他从没见过它这样冷落。坟场一般的寂静罩着一切。他掏出怀表。全城吃茶去了，他想；他怀着轻快的心继续走，此生从未这样泰然过。
转过街角，他蓦地看见面前一大群人，夹在他和集市之间——大教堂正俯瞰着那集市；他知道必定出了什么事，把城里别处的人都引来了。走近了看，仿佛全城人口都聚在了这里。显眼的是神气活现的帕金尊牧，旁边站着帕金太太，还有高高的溜肩膀的努特先生。油光光的不奉国教牧师在场，圆脸快活的天主教神父也在场。

===Original===

There stood Joliffe, the pork-butcher, in shirtsleeves and white apron in the middle of the road; and there stood Joliffe’s wife and daughters, piled up on the steps of the shop, and craning their necks towards the marketplace. The postmaster and his clerk and two letter-carriers had come out from the post-office. All the young ladies and young gentlemen from Rose and Storey’s establishment were herded in front of their great glittering shopwindow, and among them shone the fair curls of Mr. Storey, the junior partner, himself. A little lower down was a group of masons and men employed on the restorations, and near them Clerk Janaway leant on his stick.
Many of these people Lord Blandamer knew well by sight, and there was beside a great throng of common folk, but none took any notice of him.
There was something very strange about the crowd. Everyone was looking towards the marketplace, and everyone’s face was upturned as if they were watching a flight of birds. The square was empty, and no one attempted to advance further into it; nay, most stood in an alert attitude, as if prepared to run the other way. Yet all remained spellbound, looking up, with their heads turned towards the marketplace, over which watched the minster church. There was no shouting, nor laughter, nor chatter; only the agitated murmur of a multitude of people speaking under their breath.
The single person that moved was a wagoner. He was trying to get his team and cart up the street, away from the marketplace, but made slow progress, for the crowd was too absorbed to give him room. Lord Blandamer spoke to the man, and asked him what was happening. The wagoner stared for a moment as if dazed; then recognised his questioner, and said quickly:
“Don’t go on, my lord! For God’s sake, don’t go on; the tower’s coming down.”
Then the spell that bound all the others fell on Lord Blandamer too. His eyes were drawn by an awful attraction to the great tower that watched over the marketplace. The buttresses with their broad set-offs, the double belfry windows with their pierced screens and stately Perpendicular tracery, the open battlemented parapet, and clustered groups of soaring pinnacles, shone pink and mellow in the evening sun. They were as fair and wonderful as on that day when Abbot Vinnicomb first looked upon his finished work, and praised God that it was good.

===Chinese===

猪肉商乔利夫站在街心，穿着衬衫，系着白围裙；乔利夫的太太和女儿们堆在店铺的台阶上，朝集市方向伸长了脖子。邮政局长带着他的文书和两个邮差走出了邮局。罗斯-斯托里商号全体的年轻小姐和年轻先生们，聚在他们晶光闪闪的大橱窗前，小老板斯托里先生自己那头金色的罥发也在其中闪闪。略靠下是一群泥瓦匠和修缮工程的工人，执事亚纳韦拄着手杖站在他们近旁。
这些人里有许多布兰达默勋爵熟识面孔，此外还有一大群寻常百姓；可是没有一个人留意他。
这人群有一样非常奇怪的地方。人人都朝集市望，人人都仰着脸，仿佛在看一群飞鸟。广场是空的，没有一个人想再往里进；不，多数人反而摆出一副随时预备往回跑的戒备姿态。然而人人又都像被咒定住了，仰着头，脸转向集市——大教堂正俯瞰着的那集市。没有喊叫，没有笑，没有闲话；只有一大群人压低嗓子说话的、不安的嗡嗡。
唯一动弹的是个赶大车的。他正把他的马队和车往街上方赶，离开集市，却进展缓慢，因为人群全然顾不上给他让路。布兰达默勋爵同那人说了一句，问出了什么事。车把式怔了片刻，像发懵；随即认出了问话的人，急急地说：
“别往前走，爵爷！看在上帝分上，别往前走；塔要塌了。”
于是缚住众人的符咒也落到了布兰达默勋爵身上。他的眼睛被一种可怖的吸力拽向那俯瞰集市的大塔。带着宽大承层的扶壁，双扇钟窗上镂空的屏格和堂皇的垂直式窗花，敞开的垛口女儿墙，一簇簇耸立的尖塔，在晚照里泛着温润的粉红。它们同维尼科姆院长初次瞻望落成的工程、赞美上帝说“甚好”的那一天一样美好，一样神奇。

===Original===

But on this still autumn evening there was something terribly amiss with the tower, in spite of all brave appearances. The jackdaws knew it, and whirled in a mad chattering cloud round their old home, with wings flashing and changing in the low sunlight. And on the west side, the side nearest the marketplace, there oozed out from a hundred joints a thin white dust that fell down into the churchyard like the spray of some lofty Swiss cascade. It was the very death-sweat of a giant in his agony, the mortar that was being ground out in powder from the courses of collapsing masonry. To Lord Blandamer it seemed like the sand running through an hourglass.
Then the crowd gave a groan like a single man. One of the gargoyles at the corner, under the parapet, a demon figure that had jutted grinning over the churchyard for three centuries, broke loose and fell crashing on to the gravestones below. There was silence for a minute, and then the murmurings of the onlookers began again. Everyone spoke in short, breathless sentences, as though they feared the final crash might come before they could finish. Churchwarden Joliffe, with pauses of expectation, muttered about a “judgment in our midst.” The Rector, in Joliffe’s pauses, seemed trying to confute him by some reference to “those thirteen upon whom the tower of Siloam fell and slew them.” An old charwoman whom Miss Joliffe sometimes employed wrung her hands with an “Ah! poor dear﻿—poor dear!” The Catholic priest was reciting something in a low tone, and crossing himself at intervals. Lord Blandamer, who stood near, caught a word or two of the commendatory prayer for the dying, the “*Proficiscere*,” and “*liliata rutilantium*,” that showed how Abbot Vinnicomb’s tower lived in the hearts of those that abode under its shadow.
And all the while the white dust kept pouring out of the side of the wounded fabric; the sands of the hourglass were running down apace.
The foreman of the masons saw Lord Blandamer, and made his way to him.
“Last night’s gale did it, my lord,” he said; “we knew ’twas touch and go when we came this morning. Mr. Westray’s been up the tower since midday to see if there was anything that could be done, but twenty minutes ago he came sharp into the belfry and called to us, ‘Get out of it, lads﻿—get out quick for your lives; it’s all over now.’

===Chinese===

可是这个寂静的秋晚，塔却出了大凶，任它外表怎样撑场面。寒鸦知道，绕着老家疯狂地聒噪盘旋，翅膀在低低的日光里明灭闪烁。在西面——朝集市最近的一面——上百道灰缝里渗出细细的白尘，落向教堂墓地，像某座高悬的瑞士瀑布溅起的水雾。那是一个巨人临终挣扎的汗珠，是崩坍中的砌体一层层磨出来的、粉化的灰浆。在布兰达默勋爵看来，它像漏壶里漏下的沙。
随后人群像一个人似的发出一声呻吟。女儿墙底下一个角上的滴水兽——那狞笑了三个世纪、俯临教堂墓地的魔怪像——松脱了，轰然砸在下面的墓石上。静了一分钟，围观者的嗡嗡又起来了。人人说的都是短促的、气不接的句子，仿佛怕末日的巨响会赶在他们说完之前落下。教堂执事乔利夫一顿一顿地咕哝着“降在我们中间的审判”。教区长在乔利夫的顿挫之间，似乎在引“西罗亚楼倒塌压死的那些人”反驳他。乔利夫小姐有时雇的一个打杂老妇人绞着手喊“啊！可怜的人——可怜的人！”天主教神父低声诵着什么，不时画十字。站在近处的布兰达默勋爵，捕到临终助祷辞的一两个词——“*Proficiscere*”（起程吧）和“*liliata rutilantium*”（闪耀者的百合）——可见维尼科姆院长的塔，活在它荫下居民的心里。
白尘不停地从这受伤的建筑侧面涌出；漏壶里的沙漏得急了。
泥瓦匠工头看见了布兰达默勋爵，挤了过来。
“昨夜的大风干的，爵爷，”他说，“今儿早晨一来，我们就知道悬了。韦斯特雷先生从中午就上塔去，看还有没有办法；二十分钟前他急匆匆冲进钟楼朝我们喊：‘快出去，伙计们——逃命要紧，快出去；这下全完了。’

===Original===

It’s widening out at bottom; you can see how the base wall’s moved and forced up the graves on the north side.” And he pointed to a shapeless heap of turf and gravestones and churchyard mould against the base of the tower.
“Where is Mr. Westray?” Lord Blandamer said. “Ask him to speak to me for a minute.”
He looked round about for the architect; he wondered now that he had not seen him among the crowd. The people standing near had listened to Lord Blandamer’s words. They of Cullerne looked on the master of Fording as being almost omnipotent. If he could not command the tower, like Joshua’s sun in Ajalon, to stand still forthwith and not fall down, yet he had no doubt some sage scheme to suggest to the architect whereby the great disaster might be averted. Where was the architect? they questioned impatiently. Why was he not at hand when Lord Blandamer wanted him? Where was he? And in a moment Westray’s name was on all lips.
And just then was heard a voice from the tower, calling out through the louvres of the belfry windows, very clear and distinct for all it was so high up, and for all the chatter of the jackdaws. It was Westray’s voice:
“I am shut up in the belfry,” it called; “the door is jammed. For God’s sake! someone bring a crowbar, and break in the door!”
There was despair in the words, that sent a thrill of horror through those that heard them. The crowd stared at one another. The foreman-mason wiped the sweat off his brow; he was thinking of his wife and children. Then the Catholic priest stepped out.
“I will go,” he said; “I have no one depending on me.”
Lord Blandamer’s thoughts had been elsewhere; he woke from his reverie at the priest’s words.
“Nonsense!” said he; “I am younger than you, and know the staircase. Give me a lever.” One of the builder’s men handed him a lever with a sheepish air. Lord Blandamer took it, and ran quickly towards the minster.
The foreman-mason called after him:
“There is only one door open, my lord﻿—a little door by the organ.”
“Yes, I know the door,” Lord Blandamer shouted, as he disappeared round the church.
A few minutes later he had forced open the belfry door. He pulled it back towards him, and stood behind it on the steps higher up, leaving the staircase below clear for Westray’s escape.

===Chinese===

‘底下正豁开呢；您看北边的墙根怎么一动，把坟都顶起来了。’”他指着塔基旁一堆不成形状的草皮、墓石和墓土。
“韦斯特雷先生在哪儿？”布兰达默勋爵说，“请他来同我说句话。”
他四下张望着找建筑师；他此刻才奇怪怎么没有在人群里见到他。站在近旁的人听见了布兰达默勋爵的话。卡勒恩人把福丁的主人看得几乎是全能的。他纵然不能像约书亚在亚雅仑命令日头那样，喝令塔立即站住、不许倒下，也必有一条锦囊妙计献给建筑师，好躲过这场大灾。建筑师在哪儿？他们不耐烦地质问。布兰达默勋爵要找他，他怎么不在跟前？他在哪儿？一时间，韦斯特雷的名字挂在了所有人嘴上。
正在这时，塔上传来一个声音，穿过钟窗的百叶，尽管那样高、尽管寒鸦聒噪，仍旧清清楚楚。那是韦斯特雷的声音：
“我被关在钟楼里了，”它喊道，“门卡死了。看在上帝分上！谁去拿根撬棍，把门砸开！”
话音里透着绝望，叫听见的人浑身恐怖一震。人群面面相觑。工头泥瓦匠抹去额头上的汗；他想起了老婆孩子。于是天主教神父站了出来。
“我去，”他说，“我没有家小要靠我。”
布兰达默勋爵的心思原在别处；神父的话把他从沉思里唤醒。
“胡说！”他说，“我比你年轻，路我熟。给我一根撬棍。”营造商的一个工人咖咖地递给他一根撬棍。布兰达默勋爵接了，快步向大教堂跑去。
工头在他身后喊：
“只有一扇门开着，爵爷——风琴旁边的小门。”
“是，我知道那扇门。”布兰达默勋爵扬声应着，身影消失在教堂拐角后。
几分钟后，他把钟楼的门撬开了。他把门朝自己拉回，站在门后高几级的台阶上，把下面的楼梯让空出来，好让韦斯特雷逃生。

===Original===

The eyes of the two men did not meet, for Lord Blandamer was hidden by the door; but Westray was much overcome as he thanked the other for rescuing him.
“Run for your life!” was all Lord Blandamer said; “you are not saved yet.”
The younger man dashed headlong down the steps, and then Lord Blandamer pushed the door to, and followed with as little haste or excitement as if he had been coming down from one of his many inspections of the restoration work.
As Westray ran through the great church, he had to make his way through a heap of mortar and debris that lay upon the pavement. The face of the wall over the south transept arch had come away, and in its fall had broken through the floor into the vaults below. Above his head that baleful old crack, like a black lightning-flash, had widened into a cavernous fissure. The church was full of dread voices, of strange moanings and groanings, as if the spirits of all the monks departed were wailing for the destruction of Abbot Vinnicomb’s tower. There was a dull rumbling of rending stone and crashing timbers, but over all the architect heard the cry of the crossing-arches: “The arch never sleeps, never sleeps. They have bound upon us a burden too heavy to be borne; we are shifting it. The arch never sleeps.”
Outside, the people in the marketplace held their breath, and the stream of white dust still poured out of the side of the wounded tower. It was six o’clock; the four quarters sounded, and the hour struck. Before the last stroke had died away Westray ran out across the square, but the people waited to cheer until Lord Blandamer should be safe too. The chimes began “Bermondsey” as clearly and cheerfully as on a thousand other bright and sunny evenings.
And then the melody was broken. There was a jangle of sound, a deep groan from Taylor John, and a shrill cry from Beata Maria, a roar as of cannon, a shock as of an earthquake, and a cloud of white dust hid from the spectators the ruin of the fallen tower.

===Chinese===

两人的目光没有相遇，因为布兰达默勋爵藏在门后；可韦斯特雷感激他救命之恩时，激动得说不出所以。
“快逃命去！”布兰达默勋爵只说了这一句，“你还没有得救呢。”
年轻人没命地冲下台阶；随后布兰达默勋爵掩上门，跟了下来，从容得不慌不忙，仿佛只是从他无数次修缮巡查中的一次走下来。
韦斯特雷跑过大教堂时，得从摊在中堂地面上的一堆灰浆和碎砾中间穿过。南耳堂拱上方的墙面剥落了，坠落时砸穿了地面，直透进底下的墓窟。他头顶上，那道凶恶的老裂缝，像一道黑色的闪电，已经豁成了洞穴般的大口。教堂里满是可怖的声音，奇怪的呜咽和呻吟，仿佛历代修士的亡灵都在为维尼科姆院长的塔之将毁而哀哭。石块崩裂、木料坍折的闷响滚滚不绝；可越过这一切，建筑师听得见交叉拱的呼喊：“拱从不睡觉，从不睡觉。他们捆在我们身上的担子重得扛不动了；我们正在卸掉它。拱从不睡觉。”
外面，集市上的人们屏住呼吸，白尘的细流仍旧从受伤的塔身涌出。六点钟了；四刻报过，正点敲响。末一声钟响还没散尽，韦斯特雷已经跑着穿过广场；人们却憋着欢呼，要等布兰达默勋爵也脱险。钟琴奏起《伯蒙德西》，同过去一千个晴朗明媚的傍晚一样清亮、一样欢快。
随后乐音断了。一阵杂乱的巨响，“裁缝约翰”一声深长的呻吟，“蒙福马利亚”一声尖利的哀叫，一阵炮击似的轰鸣，一阵地震似的震动，一团白尘腾起，把倒塌的高塔的废墟，从围观者的眼前遮没了。
